\quad J_{2}&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(1+B\,|\widehat{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\max\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\}/Q^{\prime})^{A_{1}}\end{split}

are defined in terms of the convenient quantity
Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q_{1}Q_{0}
.
Since
I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is supported on a range of the form
q\ll Q
,
we may assume that
Q^{\prime}\ll Q=B^{3/2}
.

Next, write
q_{0}=q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}
,
where
q_{2}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
with

\gcd(q_{2},\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))=1,

where
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
with

\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}),

and where

p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})>v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})).

On observing that
\{q,m_{i}\}
is a square-full integer
d_{i}\mid\gcd(q,m_{i})
,
we find that

(6.4)

\begin{split}E_{1,2}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\geqslant 1\\
\textnormal{square-full}\end{subarray}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{J_{1}}{Q_{1}^{3}J_{2}Q_{0}^{6}}\left\lvert\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\frac{S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q_{1}^{3}}\right\rvert\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}\sim Q_{0}\\
q_{2},q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
d_{i}\mid q_{3}\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}q_{2}^{4}q_{3}^{4+\varepsilon}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1/2}\frac{q_{4}^{4}}{\kappa(q_{4})}.\end{split}

Let
Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}\gg 1
such that
Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}\asymp Q_{0}
.
Let

E_{1,3}=E_{1,3}(B,Q_{4},\dots,Q_{1})

be
the contribution
to the right-hand side from

q_{2}\sim Q_{2}
,
q_{3}\sim Q_{3}
and
q_{4}\sim Q_{4}
. Then

(6.5)

\begin{split}E_{1,3}\ll\frac{Q_{2}^{4}Q_{3}^{4+\varepsilon}Q_{4}^{4}}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{0}^{6}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\geqslant 1\\
\textnormal{square-full}\end{subarray}}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1/2}\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{J_{1}}{J_{2}}\left\lvert\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\frac{S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{Q_{1}q_{1}^{3}}\right\rvert\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{j}\sim Q_{j},\;(2\leqslant j\leqslant 4)\\
q_{2},q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
d_{i}\mid q_{3}\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{1}{\kappa(q_{4})}.\end{split}

In view of the convergence of the series

\sum_{\textnormal{square-full }d\geqslant 1}d^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
,
it will be convenient to use the inequality
d_{i}\leqslant q_{3}
to write

(6.6)

\begin{split}E_{1,3}\ll\frac{Q_{2}^{4}Q_{3}^{4+7\varepsilon}Q_{4}^{4}}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{0}^{6}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\geqslant 1\\
\textnormal{square-full}\end{subarray}}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon}\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{J_{1}}{J_{2}}\left\lvert\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\frac{S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{Q_{1}q_{1}^{3}}\right\rvert\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{j}\sim Q_{j},\;(2\leqslant j\leqslant 4)\\
q_{2},q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
d_{i}\mid q_{3}\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{1}{\kappa(q_{4})}.\end{split}

At this point, the extreme case
Q^{\prime}\asymp Q_{1}
is a real analysis and large sieve problem,
the case
Q^{\prime}\asymp Q_{2}
is a Hooley
\Delta
-function problem,
the case
Q^{\prime}\asymp Q_{3}
is an Ekedahl sieve problem,
and the case
Q^{\prime}\asymp Q_{4}
is a divisor function problem.
In general, we combine these ingredients in a suitable way,
based on the relative sizes of
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}
.

We can handle the aspects
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
Q_{3}
, and
Q_{4}

by taking large moments
in Hölder’s inequality over
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
which will have the advantage of separating the variables
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}
.
Recall the definition (
6.3
) of
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
and fix
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
.
For any
\delta\geqslant 0
, define

\Sigma_{1}^{\delta}(\mathbf{d})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\sim Q_{2}\\
q_{2}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1\right)^{1+\delta}J_{1}.

Next, for any
A\geqslant 0
, we let

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{J_{1}}{J_{2}^{A}},

(6.7)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A}J_{1},

(6.8)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1\right)^{A}J_{1},

and

\Sigma_{5}^{A}(\mathbf{d})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{4}\sim Q_{4}\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\kappa(q_{4})}\right)^{A}J_{1}

With this notation it now follows that the sum over
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}
in (
6.6
) is

(6.9)

\leqslant\Sigma_{1}^{\delta}(\mathbf{d})^{1/(1+\delta)}\prod_{2\leqslant i\leqslant 5}\Sigma_{i}^{4A}(\mathbf{d})^{1/(4A)},

provided that
\delta,A>0
are reals with
1=\frac{1}{1+\delta}+\frac{1}{A}
.
This leads us
prove the following five lemmas,
where
\log_{+}{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\max\{1,\log{x}\}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\delta>0
and assume that
A_{1}>4
. Then

\Sigma_{1}^{\delta}(\mathbf{d})\ll_{\delta,A_{1}}\frac{B^{3}(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}}.

Proof.

Putting
u=\log Q_{2}
, we see that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\sim Q_{2}\\
q_{2}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{u}<q_{2}\leqslant e^{1+u}\\
q_{2}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\Delta(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})),

where
\Delta(n)
is Hooley’s
\Delta
-function, as defined in
(
7.1
).
Breaking the sum over
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}
into dyadic intervals, we find that

(6.10)

\Sigma_{1}^{\delta}(\mathbf{d})\ll\sum_{T}\frac{(BT/Q^{\prime})^{-2}}{(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}}S_{T},

where

S_{T}
is defined in
(
7.2
).
It now follows from Lemma
7.2
that

S_{T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
\max\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\}\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\Delta(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))^{1+\delta}\ll_{\delta}\frac{T^{6}(\log{T})^{3\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}

if
d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}\leqslant T^{1/3}
, say.
On the other hand, the same bound on
S_{T}

holds trivially by the divisor bound if
d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}>T^{1/3}
.
Plugging this bound into (
6.10
), we get

\Sigma_{1}^{\delta}(\mathbf{d})\ll_{\delta}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{\varepsilon-1}\sum_{T}\frac{T^{6}(\log{T})^{3\delta}}{(BT/Q^{\prime})^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}}\ll_{\delta,A_{1}}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{\varepsilon-1}\frac{B^{3}(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}}{(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}},

upon summing separately over the ranges
T\leqslant B^{1/2}

and
T\geqslant B^{1/2}
, provided
A_{1}>4
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
A>0
and assume that
A_{1}>6
. Then

\Sigma_{2}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll_{A,A_{1}}\frac{B^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2+\min(A_{1}A,1/5)}}.

Proof.

Let
L\leqslant 1\leqslant T
and suppose that

\max\{\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert,\lvert\mathbf{n}\rvert\}\asymp T
and
0\neq|\widehat{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\asymp L
.
Then

J_{2}\asymp(1+BLT/Q^{\prime})^{A_{1}}
by (
6.3
),
and
L\asymp\left\lvert D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert/T^{6}\gg 1/T^{6}
by the definition (
3.9
) of
\widehat{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\#\{\max\{\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert,\lvert\mathbf{n}\rvert\}\asymp T:D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0,\;d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0,\;\widehat{D}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll L\}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\#\left\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T:d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0,\;\min_{\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1}\left\lvert\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}(m_{i}/T)^{1/2}(n_{i}/T)\right\rvert\ll L^{1/4}\right\},

by the factorization in (
3.7
).
Once
\mathbf{m},n_{1},n_{2}
are specified, the number of available choices for
n_{3}
in the latter count is
\ll 1+TL^{1/4}/\left\lvert m_{3}/T\right\rvert^{1/2}
,
so the last display is

\ll\frac{T^{4}}{d_{1}d_{2}}\sum_{0<m^{\prime}_{3}\ll T/d_{3}}\left(1+\frac{TL^{1/4}}{\left\lvert d_{3}m^{\prime}_{3}/T\right\rvert^{1/2}}\right)\ll\frac{T^{5}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}+\frac{T^{6}L^{1/4}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}},

where we have written
\left\lvert m_{3}\right\rvert=d_{3}m^{\prime}_{3}
.
Summing dyadically over
T
and
L
, we get

\Sigma_{2}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll\sum_{T\geqslant 1\geqslant L\gg T^{-6}}\frac{T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{-1}+L^{1/4}}{(BT/Q^{\prime})^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}(1+BLT/Q^{\prime})^{A_{1}A}}.

Since
1+BLT/Q^{\prime}\geqslant 1
and
L^{1/4}/(BLT/Q^{\prime})^{\min(A_{1}A,1/5)}
is a strictly increasing function of
L
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll~

\displaystyle\sum_{T\geqslant 1}\frac{T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{-1}\log_{+}{T}}{(BT/Q^{\prime})^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{T\geqslant 1}\frac{T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{1}{(BT/Q^{\prime})^{2+\min(A_{1}A,1/5)}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}},

where we have separately bounded the contributions from the two terms in the expression
T^{-1}+L^{1/4}
.
Since
T^{a}/(BT/Q^{\prime})^{b}
is strictly increasing in
T\leqslant B^{1/2}

and
(T/B^{1/2})^{-A_{1}}T^{a}/(BT/Q^{\prime})^{b}
is strictly decreasing in
T\geqslant B^{1/2}

for any fixed exponents
5\leqslant a\leqslant 6
and
2\leqslant b\leqslant 2+\min(A_{1}A,1/5)
,
assuming
A_{1}>6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\ll\frac{(B^{1/2})^{5+\varepsilon}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{1}{(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{(B^{1/2})^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{1}{(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2+\min(A_{1}A,1/5)}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{B^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2+\min(A_{1}A,1/5)}},

where the final inequality holds because

(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{1/5}\ll(B^{3/2})^{1/5}\ll(B^{1/2})^{1-\varepsilon}
.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
A>0
and assume that
A_{1}>6
. Then,
for all
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\Sigma_{3}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll_{A,A_{1}}\frac{B^{3}(\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{-1/\varepsilon}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}}.

Proof.

We will use Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
.
Letting
r_{5}
be the maximal square-full divisor of
q

and letting
r_{0}=\gcd(q/r_{5},G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))
,
the definition (
4.10
) of
S^{(1)}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
implies that

\frac{S^{(1)}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{3}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{5}r_{0}r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}r_{4}=q\\
\gcd(r_{i},r_{j})=1,\;(0\leqslant i<j\leqslant 5)\\
p\mid r_{5}\Rightarrow p^{2}\mid r_{5}\\
r_{0}=\kappa(r_{0})\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\gcd(r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}r_{4},G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))=1\end{subarray}}\mu(r_{4})\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3}\mu(r_{k})(\frac{m_{i}m_{j}}{r_{k}}),

where
k=6-i-j
is such that
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.
Let
H(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and

G_{t}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&\text{ if $1\leqslant t\leqslant 4$,}\\
1&\text{ if $t\in\{0,5\}$.}\end{cases}

Suppose
r_{t}\sim R_{t}
for
0\leqslant t\leqslant 5
,
such that

(6.11)

R_{5}R_{0}R_{1}R_{2}R_{3}R_{4}\asymp Q_{1}.

Freezing all but one variable
r_{t}
,
it follows from
the triangle inequality
that

(6.12)

\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\frac{S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q_{1}^{3}}\ll\min_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 5}\sum_{q\asymp Q_{1}/R_{t}}\tau(q)^{5}\left\lvert S_{t}\right\rvert,

where
S_{t}
denotes the quantity

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{t}\sim R_{t}\\
r_{t}q\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\\
\gcd(r_{t},qG_{t}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-22.76228pt\left(\mathbf{1}_{\kappa(r_{t})^{2}\mid r_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{t=5}+\mathbf{1}_{r_{t}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}\mathbf{1}_{t=0}+\mu(r_{t})\mathbf{1}_{t=4}+\mu(r_{t})\left(\frac{m_{i}m_{j}}{r_{t}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{t=k\in\{1,2,3\}}\right).

It follows from (
4.12
) that

(6.13)

\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll\sum_{q_{1}\sim Q_{1}}\tau(q_{1})^{2}\ll Q_{1}(1+\log{Q_{1}})^{3}.

By (
6.13
) and (
6.12
),
we have

(6.14)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
H(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A}\ll(Q_{1}(1+\log{Q_{1}})^{3})^{A-1}\sum_{R_{i}}U(\mathbf{R}),

where

U(\mathbf{R})=\sum_{q\asymp Q_{1}/R_{t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
H(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\tau(q)^{5}\left\lvert S_{t}\right\rvert,

where
0\leqslant t\leqslant 5
is chosen
in terms of
\mathbf{R}

in such a way that
R_{t}=\max(R_{0},\dots,R_{5})
.
Then
R_{t}\gg Q_{1}^{1/6}
by (
6.11
).
The number of available choices for
\mathbf{R}

is
\ll(1+\log{Q_{1}})^{5}
.

Clearly
S_{5}\ll R_{5}^{1/2}
,
and if
G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
then
S_{0}\ll|G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|^{\varepsilon}
.
Thus,

U(\mathbf{R})\mathbf{1}_{t\in\{0,5\}}\ll\frac{Q_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}T^{6}}{R_{5}^{1/2}}\mathbf{1}_{t=5}+\frac{Q_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}T^{6+\varepsilon}}{R_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{t=0}\ll\frac{Q_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}T^{6+\varepsilon}}{Q_{1}^{1/12}}.

By Lemma
3.2
with
P=|G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|
and
z=q
, we have

\frac{U(\mathbf{R})\mathbf{1}_{t=4}}{T^{6}}\ll\frac{(1+\log{Q_{1}})^{2^{5}-1}}{(1+\log{R_{4}})^{B}}Q_{1}+(TQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}\frac{Q_{1}}{R_{4}^{1/2}}\ll\frac{Q_{1}}{(1+\log{Q_{1}})^{B-31}}+\frac{T^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}}{Q_{1}^{1/12}},

for any
B\geqslant 1
.
Finally, if
t=k\in\{1,2,3\}
,
then by Lemma
3.3

with
(h,m)=(m_{i},m_{j})
, we have

U(\mathbf{R})\ll T^{4}(Q_{1}/R_{t})^{1+\varepsilon}(TR_{t})^{\varepsilon}(T^{2}R_{t}^{1/2}+TR_{t})\ll T^{6+\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}(Q_{1}^{-1/12}+T^{-1}).

Choosing
\varepsilon
to be small in terms of
A
,
and letting
B=3/\varepsilon
, it follows from (
6.14
) that

(6.15)

\frac{\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T}\left\lvert\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A}}{T^{6}}\ll\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{2/\varepsilon}}+\frac{T^{\varepsilon}}{Q_{1}^{1/12-\varepsilon}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{\varepsilon}}{T^{1-\varepsilon}},

where we have included the terms
H(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0

in the summation over
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T
,
using the bound (
6.13
). Let
C(A,\varepsilon)
be the estimate

\frac{\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T}\left\lvert\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A}}{T^{6}}\ll\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{2\varepsilon}}{T}.

Clearly
C(A,\varepsilon)
follows from (
6.15
) when
Q_{1}^{A+1}\geqslant T
, and so we proceed under the assumption that

Q_{1}^{A+1}\leqslant T
.
We may assume without loss of generality that

A
be an even integer.
Let

W\geqslant 0
is a smooth function supported on a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{6}
,
and let
K\geqslant 0
be an arbitrarily large constant.
Writing

f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\left\lvert\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A},

for notational convenience, it follows from Poisson summation that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{T^{6}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}W(\frac{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}{T})f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{T^{6}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}W(\frac{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}{Q_{1}^{A+1}})f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})+O_{K}(Q_{1}^{-K})}{(Q_{1}^{A+1})^{6}},

on opening up the sum over
q_{1}
in both of the expressions on the right hand side,
switching the order of summation,
breaking into residue classes modulo
an integer of order
Q_{1}^{A}
, and then applying Poisson summation.
But then

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{T^{6}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll Q_{1}^{A+1}}\left\lvert\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}q_{1}^{-3}S^{(1)}_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right\rvert^{A}+O_{K}(Q_{1}^{-K})}{(Q_{1}^{A+1})^{6}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon}},

which is satisfactory for the claimed estimate
C(A,\varepsilon)
.

Applying
C(A/\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{2})
,
it now follows from an application of Hölder’s inequality that

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&\ll\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}1\right)^{1-\varepsilon}\left(\sum_{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{1/\varepsilon}\right)^{\varepsilon}\\
&\ll\left(\frac{T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\right)^{1-\varepsilon}(T^{6})^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon^{2}}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{2\varepsilon^{2}}}{T}\right)^{\varepsilon}\\
&\ll\frac{T^{6}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{\varepsilon^{2}}}{T^{\varepsilon}}\right).\end{split}

We now recall the definition (
6.7
) of
\Sigma_{3}^{A}(\mathbf{d})
and the upper bound
(
6.3
) for
J_{1}
.
On invoking dyadic summation over
T
, we finally deduce that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{T}\frac{T^{6}(BT/Q^{\prime})^{-2}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}}\left(\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{\varepsilon^{2}}}{T^{\varepsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{(B^{1/2})^{6}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{-2}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{(\log{Q_{1}})^{1/\varepsilon}}+\frac{Q_{1}^{\varepsilon^{2}}}{(B^{1/2})^{\varepsilon}}\right),

since
A_{1}>6
. The statement of the lemma now follows, since
Q_{1}\ll Q^{\prime}\ll B^{3/2}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
A\geqslant 0
and assume that
A_{1}>4
. Then,
for all
\delta\in(0,1/3)
, we have

\Sigma_{4}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}B^{3}Q_{3}^{-\delta/2000}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}}.

Proof.

The proof is modelled after the general Ekedahl sieve,
but also makes use of the particular structure of the variety
G=\nabla{G}=0
.
Using the derivative formulas (
4.5
),
it is easy to check that if
p\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
then
p\mid 6n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\gcd(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
.
Therefore,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\frac{T^{5+\varepsilon}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,g\geqslant 1\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
r\mid 6n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0\\
g=\gcd(\mathbf{m})\\
\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3}(m_{i}n_{i}^{2}-m_{j}n_{j}^{2})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
r\mid q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid rg\end{subarray}}1,

where the first term on the right hand side accounts for the possibility that

n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0

or
m_{i}n_{i}^{2}=m_{j}n_{j}^{2}
for some
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3
.
Now write

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,g\geqslant 1\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
r\mid 6n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0\\
g=\gcd(\mathbf{m})\\
\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3}(m_{i}n_{i}^{2}-m_{j}n_{j}^{2})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
r\mid q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid rg\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3},

where
\Sigma_{1}
denotes the contribution from
r\geqslant L
,
where
\Sigma_{2}
denotes the contribution from
g\geqslant L
,
and where
\Sigma_{3}
denotes the contribution from
r,g\leqslant L
.

In
\Sigma_{1}
, observe that

r\mid\gcd(r,6n_{1})\gcd(r,6n_{2})\gcd(r,6n_{3})

by reduction modulo
r
, so

\max\{\gcd(r,6n_{1}),\gcd(r,6n_{2}),\gcd(r,6n_{3})\}\geqslant r^{1/3}\geqslant L^{1/3}.

Since any prime
p\mid\gcd(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}),6n_{1})

divides
6(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})
, for instance,
we may replace
r
with
\max\{\gcd(r,6n_{1}),\gcd(r,6n_{2}),\gcd(r,6n_{3})\}

and permute indices
\{1,2,3\}
to assume that

\Sigma_{1}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geqslant L^{1/3}\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
r\mid 6n_{1}\neq 0\\
r\mid 6(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})\neq 0\end{subarray}}T^{\varepsilon}\ll\frac{T^{6+2\varepsilon}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}L^{1/3}},

where we have first summed over
g
and
q_{3}
using the divisor bound,
before summing over
n_{1}
,
then over
r
using the divisor bound,
and finally over
\mathbf{m},n_{2},n_{3}
.

For
\Sigma_{2}
, we first eliminate
r
and
q_{3}
using the divisor bound, getting

\Sigma_{2}\ll\sum_{g\geqslant L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
\mathrm{lcm}(g,d_{i})\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}T^{\varepsilon}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geqslant L\end{subarray}}\frac{T^{6+\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{lcm}(g,d_{1})\mathrm{lcm}(g,d_{2})\mathrm{lcm}(g,d_{3})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geqslant L\end{subarray}}\frac{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}T^{6+\varepsilon}}{g^{3}d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}},

where
g_{i}=\gcd(g,d_{i})
.
But, by Rankin’s trick, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{g\geqslant L}\frac{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}{g^{3}}\leqslant\frac{1}{L^{\delta}}\sum_{g\geqslant 1}\frac{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}{g^{3-\delta}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{L^{\delta}}\prod_{p}\sum_{e\geqslant 0}\frac{\prod_{i}\gcd(p^{e},d_{i})}{(p^{e})^{3-\delta}}.

Put
a_{i}=v_{p}(d_{i})
and relabel so that
a_{1}\leqslant a_{2}\leqslant a_{3}
.
If
a_{3}=0
then the local factor is
1+O(p^{-(3-\delta)})
. If
a_{3}\geqslant 1
then
the local factor is

\sum_{0\leqslant e\leqslant a_{1}}p^{\delta e}+\sum_{a_{1}<e\leqslant a_{2}}p^{a_{1}-e(1-\delta)}+\sum_{a_{2}<e\leqslant a_{3}}p^{a_{1}+a_{2}-e(2-\delta)}+\sum_{e>a_{3}}p^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}-e(3-\delta)}

Each of these sums is
O(p^{\delta a_{1}})
, whence

\displaystyle\sum_{g\geqslant L}\frac{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}{g^{3}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{L^{\delta}}.

The final product over
p
is

\ll\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{(\delta+\varepsilon)/(1-2\delta)}
, and so it follows that

(6.16)

\Sigma_{2}\ll\frac{T^{6+\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}.

Finally,

\Sigma_{3}\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,g\leqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
g=\gcd(\mathbf{m})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid rg\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,g\leqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid rg\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
g=\gcd(\mathbf{m})\\
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1.

Assume
L=Q_{3}^{1/1000}
, say.
Since every prime factor of
q_{3}
is
\leqslant L
,
it follows that
q_{3}
has an integer factor
f
with

Q_{3}^{1/4}\ll f\ll Q_{3}^{1/3}
, say.
Given
\mathbf{m}
, the number of available choices for
\mathbf{n}

is
\ll(T+f)^{3}(f/g^{2})^{\varepsilon-1/4}
, say,
by the univariate degree
4
case of
(
3.18
).
Thus

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,g\leqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid rg\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(Q_{3}L)^{\varepsilon}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{3}}{Q_{3}^{1/16}}L^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll L^{2}(Q_{3}L)^{2\varepsilon}\frac{T^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{3}}{Q_{3}^{1/16}}L^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{T^{3}(T^{3}+Q_{3})}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{1/17}},

where the sum over
q_{3}
is bounded using (
3.16
).

It will be convenient to
put

f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{3}\sim Q_{3}\\
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\\
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}1.

Since
\tau(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\ll T^{\varepsilon}
whenever
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T
with
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{A}

\displaystyle\ll T^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{T^{5+\varepsilon}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}+\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{T^{6+3\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}+\frac{T^{3+\varepsilon}Q_{3}^{16/17}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}},

since

\frac{T^{5+\varepsilon}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\leqslant\left(\frac{T^{6+3\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}\right)^{2/3}\left(\frac{T^{3+\varepsilon}Q_{3}^{16/17}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\right)^{1/3}.

However, if
K>0
is a sufficiently large constant
and
T\geqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}
, then

(6.17)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{A}\ll\left(\frac{T}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}}\right)^{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\leqslant Kd_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{A}.

Indeed, for any integer
\Pi\asymp d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}

and any residue class
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\bmod{\Pi}
,
there exists a pair
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\equiv(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\bmod{\Pi}

with
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\leqslant 10\Pi
such that
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
,
by the combinatorial nullstellensatz, as used in
[
10
]
*proof of Lemma 7.7.
The key point is that such a residue class
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\bmod{\Pi}

is counted
\ll(\frac{T}{\Pi})^{6}\asymp(\frac{T}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}})^{6}
times on the left-hand side of (
6.17
),
and is counted at least once on the right-hand side.

We may now deduce that

\frac{1}{T^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{A}\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta/2}}+\frac{(T^{-3}+(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A})^{-3})Q_{3}^{17/18}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}.

This is a trivial consequence of our earlier estimate if
T\leqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}
, and it follows from (
6.17
) if
T\geqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{3}^{A}
. But now, since
(Q_{3}^{A})^{-3}Q_{3}^{17/18}\leqslant 1/L^{\delta/2}
for
A\geqslant 1
,
we get

\frac{1}{T^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}f(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})^{A}\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta/2}}+\frac{T^{-3}Q_{3}^{17/18}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}},

if
A\geqslant 1
. The same bound clearly holds for any
A>0
.

We now recall the definition (
6.8
) of
\Sigma_{4}^{A}(\mathbf{d})
and the upper bound
(
6.3
) for
J_{1}
.
Appealing to dyadic summation over
T
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}^{A}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{T}\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}T^{6}(BT/Q^{\prime})^{-2}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A_{1}}}\left(\frac{1}{L^{\delta/2}}+\frac{Q_{3}^{17/18}}{T^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}B^{3}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{-2}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{L^{\delta/2}}+\frac{Q_{3}^{17/18}}{B^{3/2}}\right)

since
A_{1}>4
. The statement of the lemma follows, since
Q_{3}\ll Q^{\prime}\ll B^{3/2}
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
A\geqslant 0
and assume that
A_{1}>4
. Then,
for all
\delta\in(0,1/3)
, we have

\Sigma_{5}^{A}(\mathbf{d})\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}B^{3}Q_{4}^{-\delta/2000}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}}.

Proof.

The condition

p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2

implies, in particular, that

\kappa(q_{4})\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
Since
\#\{q_{4}\sim Q_{4}:\kappa(q_{4})=r\}\ll(Q_{4}r)^{\varepsilon}
by (
3.16
),
it follows that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{4}\sim Q_{4}\\
\kappa(q_{4})\geqslant L\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\kappa(q_{4})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\\
r\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{4}\sim Q_{4}\\
\kappa(q_{4})=r\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\\
r\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{(Q_{4}r)^{\varepsilon}}{r}.

Assuming that
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T
and
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
,
the divisor bound yields

(6.18)

\Sigma_{L}\ll\frac{(Q_{4}T)^{\varepsilon}}{L}.

In particular,
\Sigma_{1}\ll(Q_{4}T)^{\varepsilon}
.

Arguing as in the proof of (
6.16
),
we have

(6.19)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
\gcd(\mathbf{m})\geqslant L\end{subarray}}\Sigma_{1}\ll\sum_{g\geqslant L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
\mathrm{lcm}(g,d_{i})\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(Q_{4}T)^{\varepsilon}\ll Q_{4}^{\varepsilon}\frac{T^{6+\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}.

On the other hand,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
\gcd(\mathbf{m})\leqslant L\end{subarray}}(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{L})\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{4}\sim Q_{4}\\
\kappa(q_{4})=r\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r}.

Assume that
L=Q_{4}^{1/1000}
.
Since every prime factor of
q_{4}
is
\leqslant L
,
it follows that the integer
q_{4}/r\gg Q_{4}/L

has an integer factor
f
with

Q_{4}^{1/4}\ll f\ll Q_{4}^{1/3}
, say.
Since
q_{4}/r
divides
G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
, it follows that
once
\mathbf{m}
is specified, the number of available choices for
\mathbf{n}

is
\ll(T+f)^{3}(f/\gcd(\mathbf{m})^{2})^{\varepsilon-1/4}
,
by the case
n=1
and
d=4
of (
3.18
).
Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
\gcd(\mathbf{m})\leqslant L\end{subarray}}(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{L})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant L\\
r=\kappa(r)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{4}\sim Q_{4}\\
\kappa(q_{4})=r\end{subarray}}\frac{T^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}Q_{4}^{\varepsilon}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{4}}{rQ_{4}^{1/16}}L^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll Q_{4}^{3\varepsilon}\frac{T^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{4}}{Q_{4}^{1/16}}L^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{T^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{4}}{Q_{4}^{1/17}}.

It follows from this bound,
together with (
6.18
) and (
6.19
),
that

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\Sigma_{1}^{A-1}}{(Q_{4}T)^{\varepsilon}}\Sigma_{1}&\ll\frac{T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{(Q_{4}T)^{\varepsilon}}{L}+\frac{T^{3}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{T^{3}+Q_{4}}{Q_{4}^{1/17}}+Q_{4}^{\varepsilon}\frac{T^{6+\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}\\
&\ll Q_{4}^{\varepsilon}\frac{T^{6+\varepsilon}\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta}}+\frac{T^{3}Q_{4}^{16/17}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}.\end{split}

On the other hand,
the summation condition
p\mid q_{4}\Rightarrow v_{p}(q_{4})=1+v_{p}(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\geqslant 2
in
\Sigma_{1}
depends only on
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q_{4}}
,
just as the conditions
q_{3}\mid G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and
\kappa(q_{3})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
in (
6.17
) depend only on
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q_{3}}
.
Therefore, on mimicking the proof of (
6.17
),
we find that if
K>0
is a sufficiently large constant
and
T\geqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{4}^{A}
, then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\end{subarray}}\Sigma_{1}^{A}\ll\left(\frac{T}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{4}^{A}}\right)^{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\leqslant Kd_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{4}^{A}\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\Sigma_{1}^{A}.

Whether
T\leqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{4}^{A}
or
T\geqslant d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}Q_{4}^{A}
,
and whether
A\geqslant 1
or
A\leqslant 1
,
it follows that

\frac{1}{T^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll T\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\Sigma_{1}^{A}\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}L^{\delta/2}}+\frac{T^{-3}Q_{4}^{17/18}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}}.

The rest of the proof is identical to that of Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Ultimately, on plugging Lemmas
6.1
,

6.2
,

6.3
,

6.4
,
and
6.5

(with
4A
in place of
A
in the latter four)
into the upper bound (
6.9
) for (
6.6
),
we find that

E_{1,3}\ll\frac{Q_{2}^{4}Q_{3}^{4+7\varepsilon}Q_{4}^{4}}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{0}^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\geqslant 1\\
\textnormal{square-full}\end{subarray}}(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon}\frac{B^{3}H}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{1-\varepsilon}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2}},

where

\begin{split}H&=((\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta})^{1/(1+\delta)}((B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{-\min(4A_{1}A,1/5)})^{1/(4A)}((\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{-1/\varepsilon})^{1/(4A)}\\
&\qquad\times\left(\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{Q_{3}^{\delta/2000}}\right)^{1/(4A)}\left(\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{2\delta}}{Q_{4}^{\delta/2000}}\right)^{1/(4A)}.\end{split}

Note that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\geqslant 1\\
\textnormal{square-full}\end{subarray}}\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{\delta/A}}{(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}}=\prod_{p}(1+\frac{O(1)}{p^{1+2\varepsilon}}+\frac{O(p^{2\delta/A})}{p^{3+6\varepsilon}})\ll 1,

on assuming
\delta/A<1
. Hence

E_{1,3}\ll\frac{Q_{2}^{4}Q_{3}^{4+7\varepsilon}Q_{4}^{4}}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{0}^{6}}\frac{B^{3}(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}(\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{-2}}{(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{2+\eta}Q_{3}^{\eta}Q_{4}^{\eta}}\leqslant\frac{(Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4})^{4}(Q^{\prime})^{2}(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{0}^{6}(\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{2}(B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime})^{\eta}Q_{3}^{\eta/2}Q_{4}^{\eta}},

on recalling that
Q^{\prime}=Q_{0}Q_{1}\asymp Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}
,
where
\eta=\min(4A_{1}A,1/5,\delta/2000)/(4A)

and
\varepsilon\leqslant\min(1/(8A),\eta/14)
.

However, by (
6.4
) and (
6.5
),
we have
E_{1,2}\ll\sum_{Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}\asymp Q_{0}}E_{1,3}
.
Similarly, recalling the expressions for
E_{1}(B)
and
E_{1,2}

from (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), respectively,
we have
E_{1}(B)=\sum_{Q_{1},Q_{0}}E_{1,2}
.
Therefore,

E_{1}(B)\ll\sum_{Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}\ll B^{3/2}}E_{1,3}\ll\sum_{Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}\ll B^{3/2}}\frac{(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}}{(\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{2}Q_{5}^{\eta}Q_{3}^{\eta/2}Q_{4}^{\eta}},

where
Q_{5}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}B^{3/2}/(Q_{1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4})\asymp B^{3/2}/Q^{\prime}\gg 1
.
Since
Q_{2}
is determined by the quantities
Q_{1},Q_{3},Q_{4},Q_{5}\gg 1
,
we conclude that

E_{1}(B)\ll\sum_{Q_{1},Q_{3},Q_{4},Q_{5}\gg 1}\frac{(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta}}{(\log_{+}{Q_{1}})^{2}Q_{5}^{\eta}Q_{3}^{\eta/2}Q_{4}^{\eta}}\ll(\log_{+}{B})^{3\delta},

since
\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{(2^{k})^{\eta}}
and
\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\ll 1
.
Taking
\delta\to 0
, we conclude the proof of the
following result, which upper bounds the quantity
E_{1}(B)
defined in (
3.13
).

Proposition 6.6
.

For all
B\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have

E_{1}(B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\\
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{q^{6}}S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll(1+\log{B})^{\varepsilon}.

7.
Application of the Hooley
\Delta
-function

The

Hooley
\Delta
-function
is defined to be

(7.1)

\Delta(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\max_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\#\left\{d\in\mathbb{N}:d\mid n\text{ and }e^{u}<d\leqslant e^{1+u}\right\},

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We extend this to all integers by writing
\Delta(-n)=\Delta(n)
and
\Delta(0)=0
. We have

\Delta(mn)\leqslant\Delta(m)\tau(n),

for any integers
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
by
[
22
, Lemma 61.1]
.
The purpose of this section is to estimate the sum

(7.2)

S_{T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0\\
d_{i}\mid m_{i}\neq 0\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\Delta(G(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))^{1+\delta},

for any real
\delta>0
.
The sum
S_{T}
vanishes unless
d_{i}\leqslant T
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
, but we shall proceed under the assumption that
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\leqslant\sqrt{T}
.

There is an extensive literature on
upper bounds for
averages of non-negative arithmetic functions over the values of polynomials, as
found recently in the recent work of
Chan–Koymans–Pagano–Sofos
[
13
]
, and la Bretèche–Tenenbaum
[
8
]
.
We will require upper bounds that have sufficient uniformity in the polynomial involved and so it is the latter work that is more appropriate to our needs.
To begin with, we
note that

F(n)=\Delta(n)^{1+\delta}

belongs to the class of non-negative arithmetic functions considered
in
[
8
, § 2]
.
We would like to apply
[
8
, Thm. 3.1]
with
k=1
and
t=6
.
The vectors
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
with

n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0
contribute
O(T^{5+\varepsilon})
to
S_{T}
, for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Making the change of variables
m_{i}=d_{i}m_{i}^{\prime}
, and taking into account the possible signs of
m_{i}^{\prime},n_{i}
, we deduce that

(7.3)

S_{T}=2^{3}\sum_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\\
0<m_{i}^{\prime}\leqslant T/d_{i}\\
0<n_{i}\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\Delta(G_{\mathbf{d}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}))^{1+\delta}+O(T^{5+\varepsilon}),

where

G_{\mathbf{d}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}d_{i}^{2}x_{i}^{2}y_{i}^{4}-2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}d_{i}d_{j}x_{i}x_{j}y_{i}^{2}y_{j}^{2}.

For a fixed choice of
\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3})
, we now focus on estimating the sum

S=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\\
0<u_{j}\leqslant X_{j},~(1\leqslant j\leqslant 6)\end{subarray}}\Delta(G_{\mathbf{d}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{u}))^{1+\delta},

with

X_{j}=\begin{cases}T/d_{j}&\text{ if $1\leqslant j\leqslant 3$,}\\
T&\text{ if $4\leqslant j\leqslant 6$}.\end{cases}

Since
\Delta(mn)\ll m^{\varepsilon}\Delta(n)
for any coprime integers
m,n
, it follows that

S\ll\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\\
0<u_{j}\leqslant X_{j},~(1\leqslant j\leqslant 6)\end{subarray}}\Delta(G(\mathbf{u}))^{1+\delta},

where
G=G^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}
is the primitive irreducible form in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]

that corresponds to taking
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}/\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
.
This is exactly the sum considered in
[
8
, Thm. 3.1]
with
Y_{j}=X_{j}
.
Suppose that
T\gg 1
and note
that the conditions in
[
8
, Eq. (3.2)]
all hold with
\alpha=1
and
\beta=1/2
, since

\displaystyle\left(\max_{1\leqslant j\leqslant 6}X_{j}+\|G\|\right)^{1/2}\leqslant\left(T+\max\{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\ll\sqrt{T}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{T}{\max\{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\}}

\displaystyle=\min_{1\leqslant j\leqslant 6}X_{j},

under our assumption that
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\leqslant\sqrt{T}
.

Let

\rho_{G}(s)=\#\left\{\mathbf{u}\in(\mathbb{Z}/s\mathbb{Z})^{6}:G(\mathbf{u})\equiv 0\bmod{s}\right\},

for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
Taking
g=6

in
[
8
, Thm. 3.1]
and applying
[
8
, Eq. (3.5)]
,
it therefore follows that

(7.4)

S\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})^{\varepsilon}T^{6}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbb{N}\\
s\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta(s)^{1+\delta}\rho_{G}(s)}{s^{6}}\prod_{6<p\leqslant\sqrt{T}}\left(1-\frac{\rho_{G}(p)}{p^{6}}\right),

if
T\gg 1
,
where we have taken
x=T/\max\{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\}\geqslant\sqrt{T}
in
the product over primes in
[
8
, Thm. 3.1]
.
In fact this result holds for any
T\geqslant 1
, since it is trivially true when
T\ll 1
.
The following result collects together what we shall need about the function
\rho_{G}(s)
, which it suffices to understand at prime powers
s=p^{j}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\bm{\varepsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{3}
, let
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
such that
\gcd(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=1
, and put

G=G^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{d}}
.
Let
p^{j}
be a
prime power.
If
j\geqslant 2
then

p^{-6j}\rho_{G}(p^{j})\ll\min\left\{p^{-2},(j+1)^{5}p^{-j/6}\right\},

for an absolute implied constant.
Furthermore, we have

\rho_{G}(p)=\begin{cases}p^{5}+O(p^{9/2})&\text{ if $p\nmid d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}$,}\\
p^{5}+O(p^{4})&\text{ if $p\mid d_{i}$ and $p\nmid d_{j}d_{k}$,}\\
2p^{5}+O(p^{4})&\text{ if $p\mid\gcd(d_{i},d_{j})$ and $p\nmid d_{k}$,}\end{cases}

for some permutation
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

Since
G
is irreducible and has content
c(G)=1
, the bound

p^{-6j}\rho_{G}(p^{j})\leqslant p^{-12}\rho_{G}(p^{2})\ll p^{-2}

is straightforward. Moreover,
the bound

p^{-6j}\rho_{G}(p^{j})\leqslant 6^{6}(j+1)^{5}p^{-j/6}
is a direct consequence of (
3.18
) with
d=6
.
Suppose now that
j=1
.
The case
p=2
is trivial and so we can assume that
p
is odd.
If
p\nmid d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}
, then
G
is irreducible over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
and it follows from the Lang–Weil estimate that

\rho_{G}(p)=p^{5}+O(p^{9/2})
. If
p\mid d_{1}
but
p\nmid d_{2}d_{3}
, say, then

G(\mathbf{u})\equiv(d_{2}u_{2}u_{5}^{2}\pm d_{3}u_{3}u_{6}^{2})^{2}\bmod{p},

for an appropriate sign
\pm
depending on
\bm{\varepsilon}
. In this case clearly
\rho_{G}(p)=p^{5}+O(p^{4}).

Finally, we get
\rho_{G}(p)=2p^{5}+O(p^{4})
if
p\mid(d_{1},d_{2})
and
p\nmid d_{3}
, say.
∎

It follows from the second part of Lemma
7.1
that

\rho_{G}(p)\geqslant p^{5}+O(p^{9/2})
, for any prime
p
. Hence

(7.5)

\prod_{6<p\leqslant\sqrt{T}}\left(1-\frac{\rho_{G}(p)}{p^{6}}\right)\leqslant\prod_{6<p\leqslant\sqrt{T}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)\ll\frac{1}{\log T}

in (
7.4
).
Any integer
s
can be written
s=ab
, for coprime integers
a,b
such that
a
is square-free and
b
is square-full. Since
\Delta(s)\leqslant\Delta(a)\tau(b)
, we deduce that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbb{N}\\
s\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta(s)^{1+\delta}\rho_{G}(s)}{s^{6}}\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathbb{N}\\
a\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu^{2}(a)\Delta(a)^{1+\delta}\rho_{G}(a)}{a^{6}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{N}\\
\text{$b$ square-full}\end{subarray}}\frac{\rho_{G}(b)\tau(b)^{1+\delta}}{b^{6}}.

Now

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{N}\\
\text{$b$ square-full}\end{subarray}}\frac{\rho_{G}(b)\tau(b)^{1+\delta}}{b^{6}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{2\leqslant j\leqslant 12}\frac{O(1)}{p^{2}}+\sum_{j>12}\frac{O((j+1)^{6+\delta})}{p^{j/6}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)^{O(1)}

\displaystyle\ll 1,

by Lemma
7.1
.
Let
D=\gcd(d_{1},d_{2})\gcd(d_{1},d_{3})\gcd(d_{2},d_{3})
.
Hence, on applying the second part of
Lemma
7.1
, we easily deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbb{N}\\
s\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta(s)^{1+\delta}\rho_{G}(s)}{s^{6}}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathbb{N}\\
a\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu^{2}(a)\Delta(a)^{1+\delta}}{a}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid a\\
p\nmid D\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{O(1)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid\gcd(a,D)\end{subarray}}\left(2+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{O(1)}

\displaystyle\ll 2^{O(\omega(D))}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathbb{N}\\
a\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu^{2}(a)\Delta(a)^{1+\delta}}{a}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid a\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{O(1)}.

We have
2^{O(\omega(D))}\ll D^{\varepsilon}
. Furthermore, the function

g(a)=\mu^{2}(a)\tau(a)^{\delta}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid a\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{O(1)}

clearly belongs to the class of functions considered in
[
7
]
, with
A=O_{\delta}(1)

and
y=2^{\delta}
. Hence it follows from
[
7
, Thm. 1.1]
that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathbb{N}\\
a\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(a)\Delta(a)^{1+\delta}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid a\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{O(1)}\ll_{\delta}T(\log T)^{2^{1+\delta}-2}\exp\left(c\sqrt{\log\log T}\right),

for any
c>\sqrt{2}\log 2
. Applying partial summation, it follows that

(7.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbb{N}\\
s\leqslant 6T\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta(s)^{1+\delta}\rho_{G}(s)}{s^{6}}\ll_{\delta}D^{\varepsilon}(\log T)^{2^{1+\delta}-1+\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Note that
2^{1+\delta}-2+\varepsilon=2(2^{\delta}-1)+\varepsilon=2\delta\log 2+O(\delta^{2})
,
if
\varepsilon
is taken to be sufficiently small in terms of
\delta
.
Finally, we combine (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) in
(
7.4
) and then (
7.3
), in order to deduce the following result.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\delta>0
.
Assume that
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\leqslant\sqrt{T}
. Then

S_{T}\ll_{\delta}\frac{D^{\varepsilon}T^{6}(\log T)^{2\delta\log 2+O(\delta^{2})}}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}+T^{5+\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
where
D=\gcd(d_{1},d_{2})\gcd(d_{1},d_{3})\gcd(d_{2},d_{3})
.

8.
The treatment of
E_{2}(B)

Recall the definition of
E_{2}(B)
from (
3.14
), which we shall estimate as follows.

Proposition 8.1
.

We have

E_{2}(B)\ll B^{-1/4+\varepsilon}.

There are two cases we need to consider:
(1)
m_{1}=0
,
m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0
,
and (2)
m_{1}=m_{2}=0
,
m_{3}\neq 0
.
It follows from Lemma
3.1
that

\displaystyle I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll_{A}\frac{q^{2}}{B^{2}\max\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\max\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\}}{B^{1/2}}\right)^{-A},

for any
A>0
. For square-free
q
we have,
by Corollary
4.10
,

\displaystyle S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{3+\varepsilon}\gcd(q,D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})).

For
q
square-full, we will use
Lemma
4.11
and Corollary
4.4
in case (1),
and additionally Lemma
8.2
in case (2).

Let us consider the contribution
E_{2,1}
to
E(B)
from
m_{1}=0
,
m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0
.
In this case

D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})^{2}
is free of
n_{1}
, by
(
3.7
).
Placing
\max\{|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\}
into dyadic intervals of order
T
, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2,1}\ll~

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\gg 1\\
m_{2},m_{3},n_{2},n_{3}\ll T\\
m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0\\
m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}\\
q_{2}\;\text{square-full}\\
q_{2}\mid(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})^{4}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\ll T\\
\{q_{2},0\}^{1/2}\mid n_{1}\\
q_{1}\ll B^{3/2}/q_{2}\\
q_{1}\;\text{square-free}\end{subarray}}\frac{B^{\varepsilon}\gcd(q_{1},D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\sqrt{\{q_{2},0\}\{q_{2},m_{2}\}\{q_{2},m_{3}\}}}{q_{1}B^{2}T^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}.

Summing over
q_{1}
using (
3.17
),
then over
n_{1}
using the condition
\{q_{2},0\}^{1/2}\mid n_{1}
, gives

E_{2,1}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\gg 1\\
m_{2},m_{3},n_{2},n_{3}\ll T\\
m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0\\
m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}\\
q_{2}\;\text{square-full}\\
q_{2}\mid(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})^{4}\end{subarray}}(\{q_{2},0\}^{1/2}+T)\frac{(BT)^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\{q_{2},m_{2}\}\{q_{2},m_{3}\}}}{B^{2}T^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}.

Next we observe that

\{q_{2},m_{j}\}\mid\{(m_{2}n_{2}^{2}-m_{3}n_{3}^{2})^{4},m_{j}\}

for
j\in\{2,3\}
.
Hence

\sqrt{\{q_{2},m_{2}\}\{q_{2},m_{3}\}}\ll\{q_{2},m_{2}\}+\{q_{2},m_{3}\}\ll\{m_{3}^{4}n_{3}^{8},m_{2}\}+\{m_{2}^{4}n_{2}^{8},m_{3}\}.

The number of choices for
q_{2}
is
O(T^{\varepsilon})
, by the divisor bound.
Noting that
\{q_{2},0\}\leqslant q_{1}\ll B^{3/2}
, we see that

E_{2,1}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\gg 1\\
m_{3},n_{2}\ll T\\
m_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(B^{3/4}+T)\frac{(BT)^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}T^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}\sum_{n_{3}\ll T}\sum_{0\neq m_{2}\ll T}\{m_{3}^{4}n_{3}^{8},m_{2}\}.

The sum over
m_{2}

is
\ll T^{1+\varepsilon}+T^{2}\mathbf{1}_{n_{3}=0}
, by (
3.17
),
so the sum over
n_{3}
is
\ll T^{2+\varepsilon}
.
Thus

(8.1)

E_{2,1}\ll\sum_{T\gg 1}T^{2}(B^{3/4}+T)\frac{(BT)^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}\ll B^{\varepsilon-1/4}.

Finally we consider the contribution
E_{2,2}

to
E_{2}(B)
from
m_{1}=m_{2}=0
,
m_{3}\neq 0
.
In this case we have
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=m_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{4}\neq 0
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose
m_{1}=m_{2}=0
, and
q
is square-full.
Then

\displaystyle S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{4+\varepsilon}\gcd(q,m_{3})\gcd(q,n_{3}).

Proof.

It is enough to establish the bound for
q=p^{r}
with
r\geqslant 2
.
We use Lemma
4.6
.
To estimate
N_{j}(p^{r})
, we observe that
p^{h}\mid y_{1},y_{2}
where
h=\lceil r/2\rceil
.
Hence the number of
(y_{1},y_{2})
pairs is bounded by
p^{2\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\leqslant p^{r}
.
The congruence
\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{y}\equiv 0\bmod{p^{r-j}}
implies that
once
(y_{1},y_{2})
is chosen,
the number of
y_{3}
is bounded by
p^{j}\gcd(p^{r-j},n_{3})\leqslant p^{j}\gcd(p^{r},n_{3})
.
Finally, the congruence
ay_{3}^{2}+m_{3}\equiv 0\bmod{p^{r}}
implies that
once
\mathbf{y}
is chosen,
the number of
a
is bounded by
\gcd(p^{r},y_{3}^{2})=\gcd(p^{r},m_{3})
.
The lemma follows.
∎

Employing the bound in Lemma
8.2
for square-full moduli, it follows that

E_{2,2}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\gg 1\\
m_{3},n_{3}\ll T\\
m_{3}n_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}\\
q_{2}\;\text{square-full}\\
q_{2}\mid(m_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{4})^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\ll T\\
q_{1}\ll B^{3/2}/q_{2}\\
q_{1}\;\text{square-free}\end{subarray}}\frac{B^{\varepsilon}\gcd(q_{1},D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))\gcd(q_{2},m_{3})\gcd(q_{2},n_{3})}{q_{1}B^{2}T^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}.

We can actually ignore the condition
q_{2}\mid(m_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{4})^{2}
.
Carrying out the sums over
q_{1}
,
n_{3}
and
m_{3}

using (
3.17
),
we get

E_{2,2}\ll\sum_{T\gg 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}\\
q_{2}\;\text{square-full}\end{subarray}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\ll T}\frac{(BT)^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}\ll\sum_{T\gg 1}\frac{B^{3/4}T^{2}(BT)^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}(1+T/B^{1/2})^{A}}.

As in (
8.1
),
this is
O(B^{\varepsilon-1/4})
, which thereby completes the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

9.
The contribution from the dual variety

The goal of this section is to prove the following result,
concerning
E_{3}(B)
from (
3.15
).

Proposition 9.1
.

We have

E_{3}(B)=\frac{\sigma_{\infty}}{4\zeta(3)}\log{B}+O(1).

Before proceeding, we offer some intuition for why
the contribution from vectors vanishing on the dual form make up
\frac{1}{4}
of the main term.
If
\gcd(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=h
, then
for
1\ll H\ll B
each dyadic range
h\sim H

should contribute

\asymp\sum_{h\sim H}\frac{B^{3}(B/h)^{3}}{B(B/h)^{2}}=\sum_{h\sim H}\frac{B^{3}}{h}\asymp B^{3}

solutions
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to the counting function
N(B)
.
The solutions
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
in the range
B^{3/4}\ll h\ll B
are covered by
3
-dimensional lattices of relatively low height.
Specifically, if we write
\mathbf{y}=h\mathbf{t}
, then
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Lambda(\mathbf{t})
, where
\Lambda(\mathbf{t})
is defined below in (
9.1
) and
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{y}/h\ll B/h\ll B^{1/4}
.
These lattices will arise naturally through our analysis of
E_{3}(B)
.
On the other hand,
M(B)
captures the contribution from the range
h\ll B^{3/4}
, but the reason for this is more mysterious.
For a partial explanation, consider the following interrelated facts:

(1)

prime-square moduli
p^{2}
are responsible for the polar factor
\zeta(2s-11)
of
F(s)
in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, in the sense that the restriction

\prod_{p}\left(1+\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sum_{1\leqslant r\neq 2}p^{4r+3\lfloor r/2\rfloor-rs}\right)

of
F(s)
to products of prime powers
p^{r}
with
r\neq 2
would be absolutely convergent at
s=6
;

(2)

the locus
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\equiv 0\bmod{h^{2}}
includes the locus
\mathbf{y}\equiv\mathbf{0}\bmod{h}
; and

(3)

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0}
is the singular locus of
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
.

The moduli
q
in the sum
M(B)
are restricted to the range
q\ll Q
, so terms like
S_{h^{2}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})
, which carry information about the congruences
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\equiv 0\bmod{h^{2}}
, might only be capable of detecting divisors
h\mid\mathbf{y}
with
h^{2}\ll Q=B^{3/2}
; i.e.
h\ll B^{3/4}
.
In the end, we will have

M(B)=\int_{1}^{B^{3/4}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}}{\zeta(3)}\frac{dh}{h}+O(1),\qquad E_{3}(B)=\int_{B^{3/4}}^{B}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}}{\zeta(3)}\frac{dh}{h}+O(1).

Building blocks

Define
\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{3}
to be the set of non-zero vector
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}

such that
\gcd(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=1
.
Given
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{3}
,
let
\mathbf{t}^{2}=(t_{1}^{2},t_{2}^{2},t_{3}^{2})
and let

(9.1)

\Lambda=\Lambda(\mathbf{t})=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{3})^{2}:\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}^{2}=0,\;\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{t}\mathbb{Z}\}.

Then
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{6}
is a primitive rank
3
lattice
that vanishes on the hypersurface
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
.
Its orthogonal complement is

(9.2)

\Lambda^{\perp}=\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})=\{(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{3})^{2}:\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{t}^{2}\mathbb{Z},\;\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{t}=0\}.

It is readily checked that
\Lambda^{\perp}
vanishes on the dual hypersurface.

Let
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(m_{1}n_{1},m_{2}n_{2},m_{3}n_{3})
.
The equation
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
cuts out a union of coordinate lattices
contained in the dual variety
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
.
The remaining integral points on the dual variety are characterized by the following result,
in which we identify
\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{3}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that

\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
and
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
.

(1)

If
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
,
then there exists a unique point
[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

such that
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
.

(2)

Let
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.
If
m_{i}n_{i}=0
, then
m_{j}n_{j}^{2}=m_{k}n_{k}^{2}\neq 0
,
and there exists a unique point
[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

with
t_{i}=0

such that
(m_{j},m_{k})\in(t_{j}^{2},t_{k}^{2})\mathbb{Z}

and
(n_{j},n_{k})\in(t_{k},-t_{j})\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

Suppose
m_{j}m_{k}n_{j}n_{k}\neq 0
for some
j<k
.
Then
m_{j}m_{k}
is a non-zero square.

Proof.

We have
\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}n_{i}=0
for some choice of signs
\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1
.
Since
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}\geqslant 1
.
Since
\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}\leqslant 2
.

If
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}=1
,
then there exists a point
[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

such that
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
.
Moreover,

\mathbf{t}^{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
is uniquely determined by
\mathbf{m}
.

If
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}=2
,
then the multi-set
\{m_{1}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q},m_{2}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q},m_{3}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}\}

contains at least two distinct non-zero spaces,
so by the pigeonhole principle
some non-zero space
m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}
appears with multiplicity one.
Then
m_{i}\neq 0
, since
m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}\neq 0
;
and
n_{i}=0
, since
the set of all subspaces of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
of the form
r^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}
,
where
r
is a non-zero square-free integer,
is linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(1):
If
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
, then by the discussion above,

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{1/2}\mathbb{Q}=1
.
Existence of
\mathbf{t}
follows,
as does uniqueness up to coordinate-wise scaling of
\mathbf{t}
by
\{\pm 1\}^{3}
.
To check uniqueness, let
S=\{i:t_{i}\neq 0\}

and note that if
[\mathbf{t}]\neq[\varepsilon_{i}t_{i}]
with
\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1

and
\sum_{i}t_{i}n_{i}=\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}t_{i}n_{i}=0
,
then (because
\#{S}\leqslant 3
) there exists
i\in S

with
t_{i}n_{i}=0
,
which is impossible.

(2):
If
m_{i}n_{i}=0
, then
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
implies
m_{j}n_{j}^{2}=m_{k}n_{k}^{2}
,
and
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
implies that the latter products are non-zero.
Existence and uniqueness of
\mathbf{t}
are clear.

(3):
If
m_{i}n_{i}=0
, then
m_{j}m_{k}=(t_{j}^{2}h)(t_{k}^{2}h)=(t_{j}t_{k}h)^{2}

for some
t_{j}t_{k}h\neq 0
by (2).
If
m_{i}n_{i}\neq 0
, then
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h
for some
t_{i}t_{j}t_{k}h\neq 0
by (1),
and the claim follows in the same way.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
S=2\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]
.
Given
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}
with
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
,
let

(9.3)

\Delta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}2&\text{if }\#\{i:m_{i}=0\}\geqslant 2,\\
2\prod_{i:m_{i}\neq 0}x_{i}y_{i}^{\mathbf{1}_{n_{i}\neq 0}}&\text{else},\end{cases}

be a certain element of
S
.
Then
\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
, and for all primes
p
, we have

(9.4)

\begin{split}&\left|\frac{S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{p^{4}}-1-\sum_{i<j}\left(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p}\right)\mathbf{1}_{m_{j}n_{i}n_{j}\neq 0}\mathbf{1}_{n_{k}=0}-\sum_{j<k}\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p}\right)\mathbf{1}_{n_{j}=n_{k}=0}\right|\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt\ll\frac{1}{p}+\mathbf{1}_{p\mid\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}.\end{split}

Proof.

Before proceeding, observe that
the sum
\sum_{i<j}(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p})\mathbf{1}_{m_{j}n_{i}n_{j}\neq 0}\mathbf{1}_{n_{k}=0}

is non-zero only if
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0
and
\#\{i:n_{i}=0\}=1
,
and that the sum
\sum_{j<k}(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p})\mathbf{1}_{n_{j}=n_{k}=0}

is non-zero only if
\#\{i:m_{i}\neq 0\}\geqslant 2
and
\#\{i:n_{i}=0\}\geqslant 2
.
Moreover, if
n_{k}=0
then the first sum reduces to
(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p})\mathbf{1}_{m_{j}n_{i}n_{j}\neq 0}
,
whereas if
m_{i}=0
then the second sum simplifies to
(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p})\mathbf{1}_{n_{j}=n_{k}=0}
.

Suppose first that
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0
.
If
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
, then
m_{i}m_{j}
is a non-zero square for all
i<j

by Lemma
9.2
,
so (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=p^{4}-4p^{3}

by Lemmas
4.8
and
4.9
.
If
n_{i}n_{j}\neq 0
and
n_{k}=0
, then
m_{i}m_{j}
is a non-zero square,
so
(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p})=(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p})
for all
p\nmid m_{i}m_{j}m_{k}
,
and (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{i}n_{j}
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\left(1+\left(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p}\right)\right)(p^{4}-2p^{3}).

If
n_{i}=n_{j}=0
, then
m_{k}n_{k}=0
, so
n_{k}=0
,
so (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\left(1+\left(\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{p}\right)+\left(\frac{m_{2}m_{3}}{p}\right)+\left(\frac{m_{3}m_{1}}{p}\right)\right)(p^{4}-p^{3}).

Suppose now that
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=0
.
If
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
, then (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=p^{4}-p^{3}.

If
m_{i}\neq 0
and
m_{j}=m_{k}=0
, then
n_{i}=0
, so (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=p^{4}-p^{3}.

Finally, suppose
m_{i}=0
and
m_{j}m_{k}\neq 0
.
Then
m_{j}n_{j}^{2}=m_{k}n_{k}^{2}
, and
n_{j}=0\Leftrightarrow n_{k}=0
.
If
n_{j}=n_{k}=0
, then (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2m_{j}m_{k}
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\left(1+\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p}\right)\right)(p^{4}-p^{3}).

If
n_{j}n_{k}\neq 0
, then
m_{j}m_{k}
is a non-zero square
by Lemma
9.2
,
so (
9.4
) holds with
\Delta=2m_{j}m_{k}n_{j}n_{k}
;
generically

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=p^{4}-2p^{3}.

In each case,
\Delta
matches the description given in (
9.3
).
∎

For
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}
with
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
,
let
\Delta
be as in (
9.3
)
and write

(9.5)

\frac{S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{4}}=\sum_{q_{0}q^{\prime}=q}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\sum_{q_{0}q_{1}q_{2}=q}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

where

(9.6)

\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))=1}\sum_{q_{ij}r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}=q}\left(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{q_{ij}}\right)\prod_{j<k}\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{r_{jk}}\right),

where the conventions for
q_{ij}
and
r_{jk}
are as follows:

(1)

If there exist indices
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 3
with
m_{i}m_{j}m_{k}n_{i}n_{j}\neq 0
and
n_{k}=0

(where we note that such a pair of indices
i<j
is necessarily unique),
then
q_{ij}
ranges over all positive integers.
Otherwise,
q_{ij}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1
, so that it may be ignored.

(2)

For each pair
1\leqslant j<k\leqslant 3
with
m_{j}m_{k}\neq 0
and
n_{j}=n_{k}=0
,
we let
r_{jk}
range over all positive integers.
Otherwise,
r_{jk}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1
, so that it may be ignored.

Observe that if
q_{ij}\neq 1
, then
\Delta=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{i}n_{j}
and
r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}=1
.
Similarly, if
r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}\neq 1
, then
\Delta=2\prod_{k:m_{k}\neq 0}m_{k}\in\{2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3},2m_{1}m_{2},2m_{1}m_{3},2m_{2}m_{3}\}
and
q_{ij}=1
.

Lemma 9.4
.

The quantity
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
depends only on
q
and
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q}
.
The same holds for the quantities

\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

and
S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

provided that
\Delta\in S
is fixed,
which holds for example if we fix which coordinates of
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}

are zero and which are non-zero.

Proof.

The quantity
S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
depends only on
q
and
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q}
.
Since
\frac{\phi(q)}{q}
depends only on
q
,
the claim for
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
follows via (
9.5
).
Now fix
\Delta\in S
.
Then the quantity
\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}))=1}
depends only on
q
and
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q}
.
Moreover, if the residue class
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\bmod{q}
is fixed,
then the values of the symbols

(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{q_{ij}})
and
(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{r_{jk}})

in the definition (
9.6
) of
\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

are uniquely determined by the moduli
q_{ij}
and
r_{jk}
, respectively.
The claim for
\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
and then for
S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
via (
9.5
),
follows.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

If
p
is prime then
S^{\prime\prime}_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll p^{-1}+\mathbf{1}_{p\mid\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}
.
Moreover, in general

S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textnormal{square-full }q^{\prime}\mid q\\
\kappa(q^{\prime})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{|S_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{(q^{\prime})^{4}}.

Proof.

The first sentence holds by (
9.4
).
By (
9.5
) and (
9.6
),
writing
\Delta
for
\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,

\frac{\sum_{l\geqslant 0}S^{\prime\prime}_{p^{l}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})p^{-ls}}{\sum_{l\geqslant 0}p^{-4l}S_{p^{l}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})p^{-ls}}=\frac{1-p^{-s}}{1-p^{-s-1}}\left(1-\frac{(\frac{m_{i}m_{k}}{p})}{p^{s}}\mathbf{1}_{p\nmid\Delta}\right)\prod_{j<k}\left(1-\frac{(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p})}{p^{s}}\mathbf{1}_{p\nmid\Delta}\right),

whose
p^{-ls}
coefficient is

\ll 1+p^{-1}+p^{-2}+\dots=\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}\leqslant 2
.
It follows that

|S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant\sum_{q^{\prime}\mid q}C^{\omega(q/q^{\prime})}\frac{|S_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{(q^{\prime})^{4}}\leqslant C^{\omega(q)}\sum_{q^{\prime}\mid q}\frac{|S_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{(q^{\prime})^{4}},

for a suitable constant
C>0
.
Now factor out the square-free part of
q^{\prime}
,
and the coprime-to-
\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
part of
q^{\prime}
,
leaving a square-full modulus
q^{\prime\prime}\mid q^{\prime}\mid q
with
\kappa(q^{\prime\prime})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
Since we always have

|S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant Cp^{4}
, and
Lemma
4.13
implies that

|S_{p^{l}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant p^{4l}
if
p\nmid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
it follows that

\sum_{q^{\prime}\mid q}\frac{|S_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{(q^{\prime})^{4}}\leqslant(2C)^{\omega(q/q^{\prime\prime})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textnormal{square-full }q^{\prime\prime}\mid q\\
\kappa(q^{\prime\prime})\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{|S_{q^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{(q^{\prime\prime})^{4}},

since
the number of choices for
q^{\prime}
, given
q^{\prime\prime}
, is at most
2^{\omega(q/q^{\prime\prime})}
.
The divisor bound finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 9.6
.

By Corollary
4.4
,
q^{-4}S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{3/2+\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
9.5
,
S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{3/2+\varepsilon}
.
Since
\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{\varepsilon}
by (
9.6
),
it follows from (
9.5
) that
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q^{3/2+\varepsilon}
as well.

Dyadic decomposition

Returning to (
3.15
), it follows from (
9.5
) that

(9.7)

E_{3}(B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq\mathbf{0}\\
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{0}q_{1}q_{2}=q\geqslant 1}\frac{I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{2}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).

The strategy is now similar to
[
42
]
,
except that the “main” quantity
\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}
is independent of
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
as in the setting of
[
44
]
.
We seek upper bounds in most dyadic ranges of moduli.
On the other hand,
we asymptotically evaluate the sum
when
B^{3/2}/q_{0}
is tiny
in terms of
B
and
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
This last part is similar to
[
44
]
,
except that infinitely many lattices
(whose successive minima are at most some power of
B
)
are involved rather than finitely many.

For each
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}
with
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
,
define
[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

via Lemma
9.2
if
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
,
and let
[\mathbf{t}]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[1:1:1]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
if
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
,
for notational convenience.
If
E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})

denotes the contribution to
E_{3}(B)
from

|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\sim M
,

|\mathbf{t}|\sim T
,

q_{0}\sim Q_{0}
,
q_{1}\sim Q_{1}
,
q_{2}\sim Q_{2}
,
then by Lemma
3.1
and
partial summation over
q_{1}
,
there exists an interval
I\subseteq\{q_{1}\sim Q_{1}\}
such that

E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})\ll\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\sim M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{0}\sim Q_{0}}\sum_{q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}\frac{(Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2}+BM)^{-2}}{(1+M/B^{1/2})^{A}}\left|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\sum_{q_{1}\in I}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right|,

for any
A>0
.

Lemma 9.7
.

For all
T,M\geqslant 1
we have

\#\{D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0:|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll M,\;|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\}\ll M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}.

Moreover, the point count restricted to
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
is
\ll M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}
,
and the point count restricted to
m_{3}n_{3}=0
,
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}

is
\ll\frac{M^{3}}{T}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
, then
|\mathbf{t}|\asymp 1
,
so the number of available pairs
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is
\ll M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}
.
If
m_{3}n_{3}=0
,
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
, then by Lemma
9.2

the number of available pairs
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is

\ll\sum_{m_{3}n_{3}=0}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}\ll T}\frac{M}{T^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{M/T^{2}\gg 1}\frac{M}{T}\ll MT^{2}\frac{M^{2}}{T^{3}}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}=\frac{M^{3}}{T}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}.

If
m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
, then by Lemma
9.2

the number of available pairs
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is

\ll\sum_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\ll T}\frac{M}{T^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{M/T^{2}\gg 1}\frac{M^{2}}{T}\ll M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}},

where
O(\frac{M^{2}}{T})

bounds the number of solutions
\mathbf{n}\ll M
to the equation
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{t}=0
,
viewed as a congruence modulo
\max(|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|,|t_{3}|)\asymp T
.
∎

Character sums

For
q_{1}
, Lemma
3.3
will be useful.

Lemma 9.8
.

For all
Q_{1},T,M,A\geqslant 1
and
I\subseteq\{q\sim Q_{1}\}
we have

\Sigma(I,T,M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{q\in I}\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right|^{A}\ll_{A}(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{1}^{A}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{1/6}}+\frac{1}{M}\right).

Proof.

We let
\Sigma=\Sigma(I,T,M)
and write

(9.8)

\Sigma\ll\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}+\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;n_{3}=0}+\Sigma_{n_{1}\neq 0,\;n_{2}=n_{3}=0}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}.

Within (
9.8
) we further write

\begin{split}\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;n_{3}=0}&\ll\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;n_{3}=0}+\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=n_{3}=0},\\
\Sigma_{n_{1}\neq 0,\;n_{2}=n_{3}=0}&\ll\Sigma_{m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}\neq 0,\;n_{2}=n_{3}=0}+\Sigma_{n_{1}\neq 0,\;m_{2}m_{3}=n_{2}=n_{3}=0},\\
\Sigma_{\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}&\ll\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}+\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}.\end{split}

On the pieces
\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}
,

\Sigma_{n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=n_{3}=0}

and
\Sigma_{n_{1}\neq 0,\;m_{2}m_{3}=n_{2}=n_{3}=0}
,
we have
\Xi_{q}=\mathbf{1}_{q=1}
by (
9.6
),
so that by Lemma
9.7
their total is

\ll\#\{D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0:|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll M,\;|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\}\,\mathbf{1}_{Q_{1}\asymp 1}\ll M^{3}\,\mathbf{1}_{Q_{1}\asymp 1}\ll M^{3}Q_{1}^{A-1}.

On the piece
\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}

we plug in the trivial bound
\Xi_{q}\ll q^{\varepsilon}
to get

\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}\ll_{A}M^{2}Q_{1}^{A+\varepsilon}.

On
\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;n_{3}=0}
,
Lemma
9.2
gives

(m_{1},m_{2})=(t_{1}^{2}h,t_{2}^{2}h)
,

(n_{1},n_{2})=(t_{2}b,-t_{1}b)
,

\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}=-2t_{1}^{3}t_{2}^{3}h^{2}b^{2}m_{3}
,
and
m_{1}m_{3}=t_{1}^{2}hm_{3}
. But then (
9.6
) gives

\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\left(\frac{hm_{3}}{q}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,2t_{1}t_{2}hbm_{3})=1}=\left(\frac{hm_{3}}{q}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,2t_{1}t_{2}b)=1}.

By Lemma
3.3

with
H=M/T^{2}

and
t=2t_{1}t_{2}b
,
after using the trivial bound
\Xi_{q}\ll 1
to reduce from an
A
th moment to a first moment,
we get

\begin{split}\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0,\;n_{3}=0}&\ll_{A}(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{A-1}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}\ll T}\sum_{b\ll M/T}(HMQ_{1}^{1/2}+(HM)^{1/2}Q_{1})\\
&\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{A-1}T^{2}(M/T)((M/T)^{2}Q_{1}^{1/2}+(M/T)Q_{1})\\
&\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{1}^{A}\left(\frac{1}{TQ_{1}^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{M}\right).\end{split}

On
\Sigma_{m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}\neq 0,\;n_{2}=n_{3}=0}
, we have
m_{1}=0
and
\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=2m_{2}m_{3}
, so that

\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=(\frac{m_{2}m_{3}}{q})\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,2m_{2}m_{3})=1}=(\frac{m_{2}m_{3}}{q})\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,2)=1}.

By Lemma
3.3

with
H=M

and
t=2
,
we get

\displaystyle\Sigma_{m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}\neq 0,\;n_{2}=n_{3}=0}

\displaystyle\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{A-1}\sum_{n_{1}\ll M}(M^{2}Q_{1}^{1/2}+MQ_{1})

\displaystyle\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{1}^{A}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{M}\right).

For
\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}
, we have
\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}
, so

\displaystyle\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(q,2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3})=1}\sum_{r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}=q}\prod_{j<k}\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{r_{jk}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}=q}\prod_{j<k}\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{r_{jk}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(r_{jk},2m_{i})=1},

where
i=6-j-k
.
By the triangle inequality, there exists a choice of
dyadic ranges
r_{jk}\sim R_{jk}
, where
R_{12}R_{13}R_{23}\asymp Q_{1}
, for which

\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}\ll_{A}Q_{1}^{A-1+\varepsilon}\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0}\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}\in I\\
r_{jk}\sim R_{jk}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j<k}\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{r_{jk}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(r_{jk},2m_{i})=1}\right|.

Suppose
R_{12}\geqslant R_{13}\geqslant R_{23}
.
Then
R_{12}\geqslant(R_{12}R_{13}R_{23})^{1/3}\asymp Q_{1}^{1/3}
.
We find by Lemma
3.3
,
with
H=M
,

q=r_{12}

and
t=2m_{3}
,
that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}\neq 0,\;\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle\ll_{A}(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{A-1}\sum_{m_{3}\ll M}\sum_{r_{13}\sim R_{13}}\sum_{r_{23}\sim R_{23}}(M^{2}R_{12}^{1/2}+MR_{12})

\displaystyle\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}Q_{1}^{A-1}MQ_{1}(M^{2}R_{12}^{-1/2}+M)

\displaystyle\ll(MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{1}^{A}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{1/6}}+\frac{1}{M}\right).

In the light of (
9.8
), we are done.
∎

Gains from sparsity

For
q_{2}
, the following variant of Lemma
9.7

will be useful.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}

with
|\mathbf{t}|\geqslant 1

and with
d_{i}\geqslant 1
.
Then

\#\{|\mathbf{n}|\ll M:d_{i}\mid n_{i},\;\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{t}=0,\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0\}\ll\frac{M}{|\mathbf{d}|}+\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{M^{2}}{|\mathbf{t}|}.

If
t_{3}=0
, then

\#\{|\mathbf{n}|\ll M:d_{i}\mid n_{i},\;\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{t}=0,\;n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0\}\ll\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{Md_{3}+M^{2}}{|\mathbf{t}|}.

Proof.

Assume
|t_{1}|\geqslant|t_{2}|\geqslant|t_{3}|

and let
g=\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})
.
If
\gcd(n_{i}/d_{i})=h\geqslant 1
,
then
(n_{1}/d_{1},n_{2}/d_{2},n_{3}/d_{3})=h\mathbf{u}
where
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{3}
.
It therefore follows from Heath-Brown
[
24
]
*Lemma 3,
with
X_{i}=M/(d_{i}h)>0
and
v_{i}=d_{i}t_{i}/g\in\mathbb{Z}
, that

\#\{|\mathbf{n}|\ll M:\gcd(n_{i}/d_{i})=h,\;\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{t}=0\}\ll 1+\frac{M^{2}}{d_{2}d_{3}h^{2}|v_{1}|}.

If
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
, then
h\ll M/|\mathbf{d}|
.
Summing over
h
, the first bound follows,
since
|v_{1}|\asymp d_{1}|\mathbf{t}|/g
.

If
t_{3}=0
, then
(n_{1}/d_{1},n_{2}/d_{2})=(bd_{2}t_{2}/g,-bd_{1}t_{1}/g)

for some non-zero integer
b\ll Mg/|d_{2}d_{1}t_{1}|
.
Since
\#\{n_{3}\ll M:d_{3}\mid n_{3}\}\ll 1+M/d_{3}
,
the second bound follows.
∎

For any
H\geqslant 1
and square-full integer
r\geqslant 1
, we claim that

(9.9)

\#\left\{h\leqslant H:\text{$h$ is square-full and $r\mid h$}\right\}\ll r^{\varepsilon}\left(H/r\right)^{1/2}.

To see this we note that any square-full integer
h\leqslant H
divisible by a given square-full integer
r\geqslant 1

is of the form
rh_{1}h_{2}^{\prime}
, where
h_{2}^{\prime}
is square-full,
h_{1}
is square-free,
and
\gcd(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime})=1
.
Then
h_{1}^{2}\mid rh_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}=h_{2}
, say, so that
h_{1}\mid r
.
Thus the number of possible
h_{2}
, given
r
, is

\ll\sum_{h_{1}\mid r}(\frac{H}{rh_{1}})^{1/2}\ll(H/r)^{1/2}r^{\varepsilon}
, as claimed.

Lemma 9.10
.

For any
Q_{2},T,M\geqslant 1
we have

\Sigma(Q_{2},T,M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{q\sim Q_{2}}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\ll(MQ_{2})^{\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2}(MQ_{2}+M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/3}).

Proof.

We write
q=q_{1}q_{\Delta}q_{2}
, where
q_{1}q_{\Delta}
is square-free
and
\gcd(q_{1}q_{\Delta},q_{2})=1
,
where

q_{1}\nmid\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
q_{\Delta}\mid\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
and
q_{2}
is square-full. It now follows from Lemma
9.5
that

\begin{split}\Sigma(Q_{2},T,M)&\ll\sum_{q_{1}q_{\Delta}q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
