|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{\varepsilon-1}q_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\\
&\ll\sum_{q_{\Delta}q_{2}\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{q_{\Delta}q_{2}}\right)^{\varepsilon}q_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\\
&\ll\sum_{q_{2}\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{q_{2}}\right)^{\varepsilon}M^{\varepsilon}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|,\end{split}

by the divisor bound applied to the integer
\Delta(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq 0
.

We now bound
S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
using Lemma
9.5
.
It follows from (
9.9
) that the number of possible
q_{2}
, given
q
, is

O((Q_{2}/q)^{1/2}q^{\varepsilon})
.
It follows that

(9.10)

\begin{split}\Sigma(Q_{2},T,M)&\ll(MQ_{2})^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{q}\right)^{1/2}\frac{|S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{q^{4}}\\
&\ll(MQ_{2})^{2\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2}\Sigma^{\prime},\end{split}

by Corollary
4.4
,
where we let

\Sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{q^{1/2}}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 3}\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i}\mid n_{i}},

where
d_{i}=\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}
.
Although we could include the condition
\kappa(q)\mid\nabla{G}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
here,
it turns out to be more convenient not to.

Let
d_{0}
be the largest integer such that
d_{0}^{2}\mid q
.
Fix
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
For each square-full
q
,

(9.11)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{i}\ll M\\
m_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i}\mid n_{i}}=\sum_{n_{i}\ll M}d_{0}\mathbf{1}_{d_{0}\mid n_{i}}\asymp d_{0}+M.

Moreover, the contribution to the left-hand side of (
9.11
)
from
n_{i}=0
is exactly
=d_{0}
,
and the contribution from
n_{i}\neq 0
is
\ll M
.
On the other hand,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m_{i}\ll M\\
n_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{d_{i}\mid n_{i}}=\sum_{0\neq m_{i}\ll M}\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}\ll\sum_{\textnormal{square-full }d\mid q}d^{1/2}\frac{M}{d}\ll q^{\varepsilon}M.

If
\Sigma^{\prime}_{S}
denotes the contribution to
\Sigma^{\prime}

from all
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
satisfying a given condition
S
,
then it follows,
by bounding any sum over
(m_{i},n_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}\times 0

in terms of the corresponding sum over
(m_{i},n_{i})\in 0\times\mathbb{Z}
,
that

\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}\ll Q_{2}^{\varepsilon}\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}},

and that for any fixed
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,

\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{i}n_{i}=0,\;m_{j}n_{j}^{2}=m_{k}n_{k}^{2}\neq 0}\ll Q_{2}^{\varepsilon}\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{i}=0,\;m_{j}n_{j}^{2}=m_{k}n_{k}^{2}\neq 0}.

Therefore, we deduce that

(9.12)

\begin{split}\Sigma^{\prime}&\leqslant\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}+\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{3}n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}n_{1}^{2}=m_{2}n_{2}^{2}\neq 0}+\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}\\
&\ll\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}+Q_{2}^{\varepsilon}\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{3}=0,\;m_{1}n_{1}^{2}=m_{2}n_{2}^{2}\neq 0}+Q_{2}^{\varepsilon}\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}}.\end{split}

By our calculations for
m_{i}=0
above in (
9.11
),
we have, since
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq\mathbf{0}
,

\begin{split}\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}}&\ll\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\frac{1}{q^{1/2}}(d_{0}^{2}M+d_{0}M^{2}+M^{3})\\
&\ll MQ_{2}+M^{2}Q_{2}^{1/2}+M^{3}Q_{2}^{\varepsilon},\end{split}

since
d_{0}\leqslant q^{1/2}
.

Turning to
\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}
,
we write
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
where
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0
,
by Lemma
9.2
.
Evaluating the sum over
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0

using Lemma
9.9
, we get

\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}\ll\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h\\
|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\\
h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{M}{|\mathbf{d}|}+\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{M^{2}}{T}\right)\frac{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}{q^{1/2}},

where
d_{i}=\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}.
We have

d_{3}\leqslant q^{1/2}
and
(d_{1}d_{2})^{2}=\{q,m_{2}\}\{q,m_{2}\}\mid\{q,m_{1}m_{2}\}^{2}
, whence
d_{1}d_{2}\mid\{q,m_{1}m_{2}\}
. Taking
|\mathbf{d}|\geqslant(d_{1}d_{2})^{1/2}
,
the total contribution from the
\frac{M}{|\mathbf{d}|}
term is found to be

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h\\
|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\\
h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}M}{|\mathbf{d}|q^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\ll M\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h\\
|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\\
h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{q,m_{1}m_{2}\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll M\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h\\
|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\\
h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\mid m_{1}m_{2}\\
\text{$r$ square-full}\end{subarray}}r^{1/2+\varepsilon}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{r}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll(Q_{2}M)^{\varepsilon}M^{2}Q_{2}^{1/2}T,

by (
9.9
).
The remaining contribution is

\ll\frac{M^{2}}{T}(TM)^{1+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2-\delta}=(TM)^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/2-\delta},

by (
9.13
).

For
\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{3}=0,\;m_{1}n_{1}^{2}=m_{2}n_{2}^{2}\neq 0}
,
we write
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
where
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0=t_{3}
,
by Lemma
9.2
and (
9.2
),
and evaluate the sum over
\mathbf{n}
using Lemma
9.9
,
getting

\Sigma^{\prime}_{m_{3}=0,\;m_{1}n_{1}^{2}=m_{2}n_{2}^{2}\neq 0}\ll\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0=t_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h\\
|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\\
h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})}{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}\frac{Md_{3}+M^{2}}{T}\frac{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}}{q^{1/2}}.

Taking

d_{3}\leqslant q^{1/2}
,
the total contribution from the
Md_{3}
term is

\ll\frac{M}{T}(TM)^{1+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2+\varepsilon},

by (
9.14
) in
Lemma
9.11
.
Using (
9.13
),
the remaining contribution is

\ll\frac{M^{2}}{T}(TM)^{1+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2-\delta}=(TM)^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/2-\delta}.

Since
\delta=1/6
is admissible in (
9.13
),
it follows from (
9.10
) and (
9.12
) that

\Sigma(Q_{2},T,M)\ll(MQ_{2})^{\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2}(MQ_{2}+M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/3}+M^{2}TQ_{2}^{1/2}).

We may assume
M\gg T^{2}
, or else
\Sigma=0
.
But then
M^{2}TQ_{2}^{1/2}\ll(MQ_{2})^{1/4}(M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/3})^{3/4}
and the statement of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
d_{i}=\{q,m_{i}\}^{1/2}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
.
Let
T,M,Q_{2}\geqslant 1
.
If
\varepsilon>0
and
0<\delta<1/2
then

(9.13)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
\#\{i:t_{i}=0\}\leqslant 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\geqslant 1\\
M\gg m_{i}=t_{i}^{2}h\end{subarray}}\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})}{q^{1/2}}\ll(TM)^{1+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2-\delta},

(9.14)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0=t_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\geqslant 1\\
M\gg m_{i}=t_{i}^{2}h\end{subarray}}\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})\ll(TM)^{1+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2+\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Taking
\delta=1/2-\varepsilon
and
t_{3}=0
in (
9.13
),
and multiplying by
Q_{2}^{1/2}
,
we obtain (
9.14
).
Therefore, we need only prove (
9.13
).

Proof for
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0
.

We have
h\ll M/T^{2}
.
Let
H=M/T^{2}
and

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\delta,1+\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right).

By Rankin’s trick we have

\Psi_{1,t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0}\ll\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{q}\right)^{\alpha}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{T^{3}}{t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}}\right)^{\beta}\sum_{h\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{H}{h}\right)^{\gamma}\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})}{q^{1/2}},

which is
Q_{2}^{\alpha}T^{3\beta}H^{\gamma}
times
an Euler product whose definition is independent of
Q_{2},T,H
.
It remains to show that this Euler product is convergent.

If
v_{p}(q)=e\in\{0,2,3,4,\dots\}
,
v_{p}(t_{i})=f_{i}\geqslant 0
, and
v_{p}(h)=g\geqslant 0
,
then

r_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}(\{q,t_{i}^{2}h\})=2\lfloor\min(e,2f_{i}+g)/2\rfloor.

Since
d_{i}^{2}=\{q,t_{i}^{2}h\}
, we get

v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}\left(\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2},d_{3}t_{3})}{q^{1/2}}\right)=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 3}\left(\frac{1}{2}r_{i}+f_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{2}e

and

\ell\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}(q^{\alpha}(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3})^{\beta}h^{\gamma})=\alpha e+\gamma g+\sum_{i}\beta f_{i}.

We begin with a clean general estimate.
Clearly

v\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i}(\frac{1}{2}r_{i}+f_{i})-\frac{1}{2}e\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i}(2f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}g)-\frac{1}{2}e=\sum_{i}\frac{2}{3}f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}g-\frac{1}{2}e,

since
r_{i}\leqslant 2f_{i}+g
.
Since
\beta,\gamma\geqslant 1
, it follows that

\ell-v\geqslant(\alpha+\frac{1}{2})e+\frac{1}{2}g+\sum_{i}\frac{1}{3}f_{i}.

If
e\geqslant 2
then
\ell-v\geqslant 1+\varepsilon
,
since
\alpha>0
.
In general, it is also clear that if
L\geqslant 1
then

\#\{(e,f,g):\ell-v\leqslant L\}\ll L^{5}.

It remains to show that if
e=0
and
(f,g)\neq\mathbf{0}
, then
\ell-v\geqslant 1+\varepsilon
.
But if
e=0
,
then
r_{i}=0
,
so
v=\min_{i}f_{i}
.
Since
\beta\geqslant 1
, it follows that

\ell-v\geqslant\gamma g+\beta\max_{i}(f_{i}).

Thus
\ell-v\geqslant 1+\varepsilon
, because
(f,g)\neq\mathbf{0}
.

Proof for
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0
.

In this case,

\Psi_{1,t_{3}=0}\ll\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q\ll Q_{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant t_{1},t_{2}\ll T}\sum_{h\ll M/T^{2}}\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})}{q^{1/2}}.

Let
H=M/T^{2}
.
We will take

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\delta,\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right).

(In fact the argument also goes through with
\beta=1+\varepsilon
, but there is no advantage in doing so.)
By Rankin’s trick we have

\Psi_{1,t_{3}=0}\ll\sum_{\kappa(q)^{2}\mid q}\left(\frac{Q_{2}}{q}\right)^{\alpha}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{T^{2}}{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)^{\beta}\sum_{h\geqslant 1}\left(\frac{H}{h}\right)^{\gamma}\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})}{q^{1/2}}.

If
v_{p}(q)=e\in\{0,2,3,4,\dots\}
,
v_{p}(t_{i})=f_{i}\geqslant 0
, and
v_{p}(h)=g\geqslant 0
,
then

r_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}(\{q,t_{i}^{2}h\})=2\lfloor\min(e,2f_{i}+g)/2\rfloor.

as before.
Thus

v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}\left(\frac{\gcd(d_{1}t_{1},d_{2}t_{2})}{q^{1/2}}\right)=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}\left(\frac{1}{2}r_{i}+f_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{2}e

and

\ell\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{p}(q^{\alpha}(t_{1}t_{2})^{\beta}h^{\gamma})=\alpha e+\gamma g+\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}\beta f_{i}.

Observe that

v\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}\left(\frac{1}{2}r_{i}+f_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{2}e\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}\left(2f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}g\right)-\frac{1}{2}e=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}g-\frac{1}{2}e,

so

\ell-v\geqslant\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)e+\left(\gamma-\frac{1}{2}\right)g+\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}(\beta-1)f_{i}.

If
e\geqslant 2
, then
\ell-v\geqslant 1+\varepsilon
,
since
\alpha>0
.
Alternatively, if
e=0
, then
r_{i}=0
, so
v=\min_{i}{f_{i}}
and

\ell-v\geqslant\gamma g+\beta\max_{i}(f_{i})
, since
\beta\geqslant 1
.
As in the case
t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\neq 0
, this suffices.
∎

Uniform upper bounds

We begin by recalling a classical result from the geometry of numbers.

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}
be a
shortest

basis of a lattice in a Euclidean space;
i.e. a basis for which
\max(|v_{1}|,\dots,|v_{N}|)
is minimal.
Then

\max(|v_{1}|,\dots,|v_{N}|)\ll_{N}\lambda_{N},

where
\lambda_{N}
is the largest successive minimum of the lattice.

Proof.

See Cassels
[
12
]
*p. 135, Lemma 8.
∎

The following lemma is proved using the geometry of numbers and
will help us remove certain akward factors of
M^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
A>0
be a large absolute constant.
Let
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q_{2}\mathbb{Z})^{3}
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}/q_{2}\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
N(T,H)=N(T,H;\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},c,q_{2})
be the number of tuples

(\mathbf{t},h,\mathbf{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{3},\;(\mathbf{t},h,\mathbf{n})\equiv(\mathbf{a},c,\mathbf{b})\bmod{q_{2}}

with
\mathbf{t}
primitive,
h\neq 0
, and
\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{t}=0

such that
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T
,
h\ll H
, and
n_{i}\ll T^{2}H
.

(1)

If
T>0
and
H\geqslant Aq_{2}
,
then

\frac{N(T,H)}{H(T^{2}H)^{2}}\asymp\frac{N(T,Aq_{2})}{Aq_{2}(T^{2}Aq_{2})^{2}}.

(2)

If
0<H\leqslant Aq_{2}
and
T\geqslant Aq_{2}^{2}
,
then

\frac{N(T,H)}{T^{2}(T^{2}H)^{2}}\asymp\frac{N(Aq_{2}^{2},H)}{(Aq_{2}^{2})^{2}((Aq_{2}^{2})^{2}H)^{2}}.

Proof.

(1):
We proceed to estimate
N(T,H)
.
For fixed
\mathbf{t}
,
the successive minima of
the lattice
\Lambda=\{\mathbf{n}\in q_{2}\mathbb{Z}^{3}:\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{t}=0\}

are
\ll q_{2}T\leqslant T^{2}H/A
. Moreover, if the set
\{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}:\mathbf{n}\equiv\mathbf{b}\bmod{q_{2}}:\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{t}=0\}
is nonempty
then it contains a point inside a fundamental domain of
(\Lambda\otimes\mathbb{R})/\Lambda

generated by a shortest basis of
\Lambda
,
which can be bounded using Lemma
9.12
.
It follows that
the number of available choices for
\mathbf{n}
is
\asymp C(q_{2},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b})(T^{2}H)^{2}

by the geometry of numbers.
Assume that

H\geqslant Aq_{2}
. Then, on summing over all available choices for
\mathbf{t}
,
and

\asymp H/q_{2}

available choices for
h
,
it follows that

N(T,H)\asymp\sum_{\mathbf{t}}(H/q_{2})C(q_{2},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b})(T^{2}H)^{2}.

Comparing this quantity to its value at
H=Aq_{2}
gives (1).

(2):
Given
\mathbf{t}
,
the number of available choices for
\mathbf{n}
is again
\asymp C(q_{2},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b})(T^{2}H)^{2}

by the geometry of numbers, since
q_{2}T\leqslant T^{2}/A\leqslant T^{2}H/A
.
Moreover,
C(q_{2},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b})\asymp C^{\prime}(q_{2},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})/T
,
since the set

\{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}:\mathbf{n}\equiv\mathbf{b}\bmod{q_{2}}:\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{t}=0\}

is nonempty
if and only if there exists
\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with
\sum_{i}(q_{2}n^{\prime}_{i}+b_{i})t_{i}=0
,
which is equivalent to asking that
q_{2}\gcd(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\mid\mathbf{b}.\mathbf{t}
.
But
\mathbf{t}
is primitive and
\mathbf{b}.\mathbf{t}\equiv\mathbf{b}.\mathbf{a}\bmod{q_{2}}
, whence

N(T,H)\asymp\sum_{h,\mathbf{t}}\frac{C^{\prime}(q_{2},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}{T}(T^{2}H)^{2}=\frac{C^{\prime}(q_{2},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}{T}(T^{2}H)^{2}(\#h)(\#\mathbf{t}).

The number of primitive vectors
\mathbf{t}\equiv\mathbf{a}\bmod{q_{2}}

of height
\sim T

is
\asymp C^{\prime\prime}(q_{2},\mathbf{a})T^{3}
,
since
T\geqslant Aq_{2}^{2}
.
If
\gcd(\mathbf{a},q_{2})\neq 1
then no such
\mathbf{t}

exist, whereas if
\gcd(\mathbf{a},q_{2})=1
then Möbius inversion
yields a uniform estimate

M(T,q_{2})+O\left(\left(\frac{T}{q_{2}}\right)^{2}+T\right)

for the number of
\mathbf{t}
,
with main term

M(T,q_{2})\asymp\left(\frac{T}{q_{2}}\right)^{3}\prod_{p\nmid q_{2}}(1-p^{-3})\asymp\left(\frac{T}{q_{2}}\right)^{3}.

Comparing
N(T,H)
to its value at
T=Aq_{2}^{2}
gives (2).
∎

The next stage of our argument is devoted to a study of the quantity

(9.15)

\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{q_{1}\in I}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right|^{2}\sum_{q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|,

for
Q_{2},T,M\geqslant 1
and an interval
I\subseteq\{q_{1}\sim Q_{1}\}
, where
Q_{1}\geqslant 1
.

Lemma 9.14
.

There exists an absolute constant
A>16
such that

\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}\ll\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime}/A^{2})^{3}}+1,

where if
R=(AQ_{1}Q_{2})^{A}
then

(9.16)

T^{\prime}=\min(T,R),\quad M^{\prime}=A^{2}\min(T,R)^{2}\min(M/T^{2},R).

Before establishing this result it will be convenient to establish a simpler variant in which

I
is taken to be
\{1\}
,

S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is replaced by
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
,
the vectors
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
are constrained,
and the old definition of
\mathbf{t}
in terms of
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
is relinquished.
Let

\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})\\
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}|S^{\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|.

The triples
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
occurring in
\Sigma^{\prime}

can thus be parameterised in terms of the triples
(\mathbf{t},h,\mathbf{n})

appearing in Lemma
9.13
.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
A\geqslant 1
be a large absolute constant and
let
Q_{2},T,M\geqslant 1
.
Then

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,A^{\tau}M)}{M^{3}}\bowtie_{\tau}\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2}),\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(M/T^{2},AQ_{2}))}{(\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(M/T^{2},AQ_{2}))^{3}}

for all
\tau=\pm 1
,
where
\bowtie_{1}
denotes
\gg

and where
\bowtie_{-1}
denotes
\ll
.

Proof.

Since
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T
and
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{t}^{2}h
,
there exist implications of the form

|h|\leqslant A^{-1/4}M/T^{2}\Rightarrow|\mathbf{m}|\leqslant M\Rightarrow|h|\leqslant A^{1/4}M/T^{2}.

Let
H=M/T^{2}
.
We proceed by applying Lemma
9.13

in each residue class
(\mathbf{t},h,\mathbf{n})\bmod{q_{2}}
,
which fixes the value of
S^{\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
by Lemma
9.4
,
and then summing over all residue classes.
By Lemma
9.13
(1) if
H\geqslant AQ_{2}
, and trivially otherwise, we get

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,A^{\tau}M)}{M^{3}/T^{2}}=\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,A^{\tau}T^{2}H)}{H(T^{2}H)^{2}}\bowtie_{\tau}\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,A^{\tau/2}T^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))}{\min(H,AQ_{2})(T^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))^{2}}.

By Lemma
9.13
(2) with
H=\min(H,AQ_{2})

if
T\geqslant AQ_{2}^{2}
, and trivially otherwise, we have

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,A^{\tau/2}T^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))}{T^{2}(T^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))^{2}}\bowtie_{\tau}\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2}),\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))}{\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}(\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(H,AQ_{2}))^{2}}.

Plugging the last display into the one before it, we get the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9.14
.

We may assume
M\gg T^{2}
,
or else
Lemma
9.7
implies that

\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)=0
.
For
T^{\prime},M^{\prime}
defined in (
9.16
),
with
R=(AQ_{1}Q_{2})^{A}
,
we have

T^{\prime}\leqslant T

and
M^{\prime}\leqslant A^{2}M
.
If
\max(T,M/T^{2})\leqslant R
,
then
T^{\prime}=T
and
M^{\prime}=A^{2}M
,
so the result is trivial.
Thus we may suppose that
\max(T,M/T^{2})\geqslant R
.
In particular, since
M/T^{2}\gg 1
we have

M=T^{2}(M/T^{2})\gg R,\qquad M^{\prime}\gg A^{2}(T^{\prime})^{2},\qquad M^{\prime}\gg A^{2}R.

We begin by writing

(9.17)

\Sigma\asymp\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}+\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}+\Sigma_{m_{3}=n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}.

By Lemma
9.15
, since
A^{-1}M^{\prime}>0
we have

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}\gg\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},\min(T^{\prime},AQ_{2}^{2}),\min(T^{\prime},AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(A^{-1}M^{\prime}/(T^{\prime})^{2},AQ_{2}))}{(\min(T^{\prime},AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(A^{-1}M^{\prime}/(T^{\prime})^{2},AQ_{2}))^{3}}.

Similarly, since
AM>0
we also have

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}\ll\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2}),\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(AM/T^{2},AQ_{2}))}{(\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})^{2}\min(AM/T^{2},AQ_{2}))^{3}}.

We have
\min(T^{\prime},AQ_{2}^{2})=\min(T,R,AQ_{2}^{2})=\min(T,AQ_{2}^{2})

and

\displaystyle\min(A^{-1}M^{\prime}/(T^{\prime})^{2},AQ_{2})=\min(A\min(M/T^{2},R),AQ_{2})

\displaystyle=A\min(M/T^{2},R,Q_{2})

\displaystyle=\min(AM/T^{2},AQ_{2}).

Thus

(9.18)

\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}\gg\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}.

But if
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
,
then
\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\mathbf{1}_{q=1}
by (
9.6
),
whence
S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
Thus

\begin{split}\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)&=\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}(\{1\},Q_{2},T,M)\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}\\
&\leqslant\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T,M)\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}\end{split}

by Lemma
9.2
.
Similarly,

\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})+\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}.

Let us first estimate the second term on the right.
By symmetry,

\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0}\ll\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=0}\leqslant\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=m_{1}=m_{2}=0}+\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=0,\;(m_{1},m_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}}.

If
n_{3}=m_{1}=m_{2}=0
,
then (
9.6
) implies
\Xi_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=\mathbf{1}_{q=1}

and
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=S^{\prime\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
.
Any pair
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
appears in
\Sigma^{\prime}
for at most four choices of
[\mathbf{t}]
,
since
[\mathbf{t}^{2}]=[\mathbf{m}]
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=m_{1}=m_{2}=0}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}

\displaystyle\ll\Sigma_{n_{3}=m_{1}=m_{2}=0}(\{1\},Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n_{3}=m_{1}=m_{2}=0}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

We deduce that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\prime}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}\ll~

\displaystyle(\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}})(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad+\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=0,\;(m_{1},m_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ll~

\displaystyle\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})+\Sigma^{\prime}_{n_{3}=0,\;(m_{1},m_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}}(Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime}),

where in the last step we apply (
9.17
).
However,
on applying the second part of Lemma
9.9
with
d_{1}=d_{2}=d_{3}=1
,
the number of triples
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
counted in the second sum on the right is

(9.19)

\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\ll T^{\prime}\\
(t_{1},t_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{M^{\prime}}{(T^{\prime})^{2}}\frac{M^{\prime}\gcd(t_{1},t_{2})}{|(t_{1},t_{2})|}\ll\sum_{1\ll U\ll T^{\prime}}U^{2}\frac{T^{\prime}}{U}T^{\prime}\frac{M^{\prime}}{(T^{\prime})^{2}}\frac{M^{\prime}}{U}\ll(M^{\prime})^{2}(T^{\prime})^{\varepsilon},

where
U
is a dyadic parameter,
since
there are
O(U^{2})
choices of primitive
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

such that
|(u_{1},u_{2})|\sim U
, there are
O(T^{\prime})
choices for
t_{3}
, and the number of choices for
(t_{1},t_{2})
is
U^{2}\cdot T^{\prime}/U
(because there are
O(T^{\prime}/U)
choices for

\gcd(t_{1},t_{2})
associated to a primitive representative
(u_{1},u_{2})
of order
U
).
Thus, in view of (
9.18
)
and the bound
S^{\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll q_{2}^{3/2+\varepsilon}
from Remark
9.6
,
we deduce that

\frac{\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0},\;n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}\ll\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}+\frac{Q_{2}^{5/2+\varepsilon}}{(A^{2}R)^{1-\varepsilon}}.

By (
9.6
), the sum
\Sigma_{m_{3}=n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)
,
is equal to

\Sigma_{m_{3}=n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(\{1\},Q_{2},T,M)\mathbf{1}_{1\in I}\ll M^{2}Q_{2}^{5/2+\varepsilon},

since the number of pairs
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
counted on the left hand side is
\ll M^{2}/T\ll M^{2}
by the proof of the
m_{3}n_{3}=0
case of Lemma
9.7
.

Similarly, by Lemma
9.7
and the trivial bound
\Xi_{q}\ll q^{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)\mathbf{1}_{T\geqslant R^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{M^{3}}{T}Q_{1}^{2+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{5/2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{T\geqslant R^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{M^{3}(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{5/2+\varepsilon}}{R^{1/2}}.

On the other hand, on
writing
(m_{1},m_{2})=(t_{1}^{2},t_{2}^{2})h

and
(n_{1},n_{2})=(t_{2},-t_{1})b
and
using Lemma
9.2
,
we have by expanding the square in (
9.15
)

\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0=t_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant|m_{3}|\leqslant M\\
1\leqslant|h|\leqslant M/|\mathbf{t}^{2}|\\
1\leqslant|b|\leqslant M/|\mathbf{t}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{1},q^{\prime}_{1}\in I}\Xi_{q_{1}}\Xi_{q^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}|.

Given
\mathbf{t}
, the number of vectors
(m_{3},h,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}

lying in a given residue class modulo
q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2}
is

\left(\frac{M}{q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2}}+O(1)\right)\left(\frac{M/|\mathbf{t}^{2}|}{q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2}}+O(1)\right)\left(\frac{M/|\mathbf{t}|}{q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2}}+O(1)\right)=\frac{1}{(q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2})^{3}}\frac{M^{3}}{|\mathbf{t}^{2}||\mathbf{t}|}+O\left(\frac{M^{2}}{T}\right),

since
M/T\gg 1
.
Summing over residue classes,
using Lemma
9.4
,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\\
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0=t_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1},q^{\prime}_{1}\in I\\
q_{2}\sim Q_{2}\\
(m_{3},h,b)\bmod{q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2}}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\Xi_{q_{1}}\Xi_{q^{\prime}_{1}}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}|}{(q_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}q_{2})^{3}}\frac{M^{3}}{|\mathbf{t}^{2}||\mathbf{t}|}+O\left(q_{1}^{\varepsilon}q_{2}^{3/2+\varepsilon}\frac{M^{2}}{T}\right)\right).

If
T\leqslant R^{1/2}
, then
T^{\prime}=T
and we may cancel out main terms for
M
and
M^{\prime}
to get

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{T(Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2})^{4+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{3/2}}{\min(M,M^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{8+\varepsilon}}{R^{1/2}},

since
\min(M,M^{\prime})/T\gg R/T\geqslant R^{1/2}
.
Together with our analysis for
T\geqslant R^{1/2}
, this implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}\ll~

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{8+\varepsilon}}{R^{1/2}},

for all
T
.

Recall our convention that
[\mathbf{t}]=[1:1:1]
if
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
, which implies in particular that

T\asymp 1
in this case. Bearing this in mind,
we have

\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}(I,Q_{2},T,M)=\sum_{J_{1}\cup J_{2}=\{1,2,3\}}\Sigma_{i\in J_{1}\Leftrightarrow m_{i}=0,\;j\in J_{2}\Leftrightarrow n_{j}=0}(I,Q_{2},T,M),

where
J_{1},J_{2}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
are sets.
If
\#J_{1}+\#J_{2}\geqslant 4
, then

\Sigma_{i\in J_{1}\Leftrightarrow m_{i}=0,\;j\in J_{2}\Leftrightarrow n_{j}=0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)\ll M^{2}Q_{1}^{2+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{5/2+\varepsilon}.

If
\#J_{1}+\#J_{2}=3
and
\Sigma_{i\in J_{1}\Leftrightarrow m_{i}=0,\;j\in J_{2}\Leftrightarrow n_{j}=0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)\neq 0
,
then
T\asymp 1
, so arguing as we did for

\Sigma_{n_{3}=0,\;m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}n_{1}n_{2}\neq 0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)
when
T\leqslant R^{1/2}
,
we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma_{i\in J_{1}\Leftrightarrow m_{i}=0,\;j\in J_{2}\Leftrightarrow n_{j}=0}(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\Sigma_{i\in J_{1}\Leftrightarrow m_{i}=0,\;j\in J_{2}\Leftrightarrow n_{j}=0}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{(Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2})^{4+\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{3/2}}{\min(M,M^{\prime})}.

In any case, it follows that

\frac{\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}}\ll\frac{\Sigma_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}(I,Q_{2},T^{\prime},M^{\prime})}{(M^{\prime})^{3}}+\frac{(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{8+\varepsilon}}{R}.

Plugging our work into (
9.17
)
yields Lemma
9.14
,
provided
A>16
.
∎

Let
0<\delta<1
be fixed.
it follows from Remark
9.6

that

|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|=|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|^{\delta}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|^{1-\delta}\ll(q_{2}^{3/2+\varepsilon})^{\delta}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|^{1-\delta}.

Hence,
in the notation of
(
9.15
),
it follows from
Hölder’s inequality between Lemma
9.8
(with
A=2/\delta
) and Lemma
9.10
that

\begin{split}\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{(Q_{2}^{3/2+\varepsilon})^{\delta}}&\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
1\leqslant|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|\leqslant M\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{q_{1}\in I}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\right|^{2}\sum_{q_{2}\sim Q_{2}}|S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|^{1-\delta}\\
&\ll\left((MQ_{1})^{\varepsilon}M^{3}Q_{1}^{2/\delta}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{1/6}}+\frac{1}{M}\right)\right)^{\delta}\\
&\quad\times\left((MQ_{2})^{\varepsilon}Q_{2}^{1/2}(MQ_{2}+M^{3}Q_{2}^{1/3})\right)^{1-\delta}.\end{split}

Thus

\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}}\ll(MQ_{1}Q_{2})^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/6}}+\frac{1}{M^{\delta}}\right)\left(\frac{Q_{2}^{1/2}}{M^{2(1-\delta)}}+\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right).

Replacing
(T,M)
with
(T^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and applying Lemma
9.14
, we get

\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}}\ll(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/6}}+\frac{1}{(M^{\prime})^{\delta}}\right)\left(\frac{Q_{2}^{1/2}}{(M^{\prime})^{2(1-\delta)}}+\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right)+\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}},

where
M^{\prime}\asymp\min(T,R)^{2}\min(M/T^{2},R)=\min(M,T^{2}R,R^{2}M/T^{2},R^{3})
.
We observe that
R\geqslant(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{16}
. Moreover,
we may assume
M\gg T^{2}
, by Lemma
9.7
, whence either
M^{\prime}\asymp M
or
M^{\prime}\gg R
.
Thus

\frac{\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)}{M^{3}Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}}\ll(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/6}}+\frac{1}{M^{\delta}}\right)\left(\frac{Q_{2}^{1/2}}{M^{2(1-\delta)}}+\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right),

since
R^{\delta}\gg Q_{1}^{\delta/6}
,

R^{2(1-\delta)}\gg Q_{2}^{2(1-\delta)/3}=Q_{2}^{1/2+(1-4\delta)/6}
,
and
Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}\gg Q_{1}^{\delta/6}Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}
.

Recall our dyadic decomposition of
E_{3}(B)

after (
9.7
).
It now follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality that

E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})
is

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{q_{0}\sim Q_{0}}\frac{(Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2}+BM)^{-2}}{(1+M/B^{1/2})^{A}}\Sigma(I,Q_{2},T,M)^{1/2}\Sigma(\{1\},Q_{2},T,M)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{Q_{0}M^{3}(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{1+\varepsilon}}{(Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2}+BM)^{2}(1+M/B^{1/2})^{A}}\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/12}}+\frac{1}{M^{\delta/2}}\right)\left(\frac{Q_{2}^{1/2}}{M^{2(1-\delta)}}+\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right).

Since we have
(Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2}+BM)^{2}\geqslant(Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2})^{(2-3\delta)/2}(BM)^{(2+3\delta)/2}

and
(1+M/B^{1/2})^{A}\gg(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{M\gg T^{2}}|E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})|

\displaystyle\ll\frac{Q_{0}^{3\delta/2}(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{3\delta/2+\varepsilon}}{B^{(2+3\delta)/2}(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}

\displaystyle~\times\left(\frac{(B^{1/2})^{\delta/2}}{Q_{1}^{\delta/12}}Q_{2}^{1/2}+Q_{2}^{1/2}+\frac{(B^{1/2})^{(4-3\delta)/2}}{Q_{1}^{\delta/12}Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}+\frac{(B^{1/2})^{2(1-\delta)}}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{0}^{3\delta/2}(B^{1/2})^{(4-3\delta)/2}(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{3\delta/2+\varepsilon}}{B^{(2+3\delta)/2}(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}

\displaystyle~\times\left(\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/12}}+\frac{1}{(B^{1/2})^{\delta/2}}\right)\left(\frac{Q_{2}^{1/2}}{(B^{1/2})^{2(1-\delta)}}+\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}\right),

since
3-(2+3\delta)/2=(4-3\delta)/2=\delta/2+2(1-\delta)
.
We note that
(2+3\delta)/2-(4-3\delta)/4=9\delta/4
. Moreover,

Q_{1}^{\delta/12}\ll B^{\delta/8}\ll B^{\delta/4}

and
Q_{2}^{1/2+(1-4\delta)/6}=Q_{2}^{2(1-\delta)/3}\ll B^{1-\delta}
,
since
Q_{1},Q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}
.
We conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{M\gg T^{2}}|E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})|

\displaystyle\ll\frac{Q_{0}^{3\delta/2}(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{3\delta/2+\varepsilon}}{B^{9\delta/4}(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}\frac{1}{Q_{1}^{\delta/12}}\frac{1}{Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{(Q_{0}/B^{3/2})^{\min(\delta/12,(1-4\delta)/6)-\varepsilon}}{(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}},

since
Q_{1}^{\delta/12}Q_{2}^{(1-4\delta)/6}\gg(Q_{1}Q_{2})^{\min(\delta/12,(1-4\delta)/6)}

and
Q_{0}Q_{1}Q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}
.
The number of dyadic choices of
Q_{1},Q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}/Q_{0}
is
O((B^{3/2}/Q_{0})^{\varepsilon})
.
Thus we may finally sum over
Q_{1},Q_{2}\ll B^{3/2}/Q_{0}

and take
\delta=2/9
to get the following result.

Lemma 9.16
.

If
B,T,Q_{0}\geqslant 1
, then

\sum_{M,Q_{1},Q_{2}\geqslant 1}|E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})|\ll\frac{(B^{3/2}/Q_{0})^{-1/54}}{(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}.

Secondary main terms

Returning to (
9.7
),
we are now prepared to extract a new main term.
Given
B,T,Q_{0}\geqslant 1
, consider the piece

\sum_{M,Q_{1},Q_{2}\geqslant 1}E_{3}(B;M,T,\mathbf{Q})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq\mathbf{0}\\
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}q_{1}q_{2}=q\geqslant 1\\
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{2}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}\Xi_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})S^{\prime\prime}_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).

of
E_{3}(B)
.
Let us call this piece
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{1}(B,T,Q_{0})
. Then,
on summing over
q_{1}q_{2}=q/q_{0}
using (
9.5
), we get

\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\neq\mathbf{0}\\
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}q^{\prime}=q\geqslant 1\\
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{2}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).

Here
I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0
unless
q^{\prime}\ll B^{3/2}/Q_{0}
,
by the properties of the
h
-function recorded at the start of Section
3
.
We would like to approximate
\Sigma_{1}
by the sum

\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}(B,T,Q_{0})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}q^{\prime}=q\geqslant 1\\
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{2}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}).

Lemma 9.17
.

Let
M\geqslant 1
.
Then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})\\
|\mathbf{m}|,|\mathbf{n}|\ll M\end{subarray}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}}+\mathbf{1}_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0})\ll T^{2}(M+T)^{2}+M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}+M^{2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}.

Proof.

We will make use of Lemma
9.9
.
The contribution from
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
is

\ll\sum_{\mathbf{t}}\#\left\{|\mathbf{n}|\ll M+T:\mathbf{n}.\mathbf{t}=0\right\}\ll\sum_{\mathbf{t}}\frac{(M+T)^{2}}{T}\ll T^{2}(M+T)^{2},

on enlarging the range
|\mathbf{n}|\ll M
into
|\mathbf{n}|\ll M+T
.
On the other hand, if
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}
, then
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0
,
so by symmetry it remains to consider the contribution
N
, say, from
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
,
n_{3}=0
.
If
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
, then
t_{3}=\pm 1
and
N\ll M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}
.
If
(t_{1},t_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}
, then

N\ll\sum_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\ll T}\frac{M}{T^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}\frac{M\gcd(t_{1},t_{2})}{|(t_{1},t_{2})|},

where the condition
M\gg T^{2}
comes from
\mathbf{m}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
Arguing as in (
9.19
), we get
N\ll M^{2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}
.
∎

By Lemma
9.2
,
Remark
9.6
,
and the bound
I_{q}\ll(1+|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|/B^{1/2})^{-A}
from
Lemma
3.1
, we see that

\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}
is

\ll\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\\
q^{\prime}\ll B^{3/2}/Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{(q^{\prime})^{3/2+\varepsilon}}{Q_{0}^{2}(q^{\prime})^{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}\\
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0\\
D(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})=0\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})\\
\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}\textnormal{ or }n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\Bigg)\left(1+\frac{|(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})|}{B^{1/2}}\right)^{-A}\hskip-5.69046pt.

By Lemmas
9.7
and
9.17
,
and the inequality
M^{2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}\ll\frac{M^{3}}{T}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}
,
the inner sum is

\ll\sum_{M\geqslant 1}\frac{M^{3}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}+\frac{M^{3}}{T}\mathbf{1}_{M\gg T^{2}}+T^{2}(M+T)^{2}}{(1+M/B^{1/2})^{A}}\ll B^{3/2}\mathbf{1}_{T\asymp 1}+\frac{B^{3/2}}{T}+T^{2}(B+T^{2}).

Therefore,

(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})(B,T,Q_{0})\ll\frac{(B^{3/2}/Q_{0})^{1/2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{0}\ll B^{3/2}}}{Q_{0}}\left(\frac{B^{3/2}}{T}+T^{2}(B+T^{2})\right).

Let
P=P(B,T)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\min(T,B^{1/2}/T^{2})^{\delta}
,
where
\delta\in(0,1)
is fixed.
If
B^{3/2}/Q_{0}\ll P
, then

\displaystyle(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})(B,T,Q_{0})\ll\frac{P^{3/2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{P\gg 1}}{T}+\frac{P^{1/2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{P\gg 1}}{B^{3/2}/P}T^{2}B

\displaystyle\ll\frac{P^{3/2+\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{P\gg 1}}{\min(T,B^{1/2}/T^{2})}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\mathbf{1}_{P\gg 1}}{P^{\delta}},

since
P^{3/2}/P^{1/\delta}\leqslant 1/P^{\delta}
if
\delta
is small enough.
Since
\#\{Q_{0}:B^{3/2}/P\ll Q_{0}\ll B^{3/2}\}\ll P^{\varepsilon}

and
\#\{T:P(B,T)\sim P\}\ll_{\delta}1
, we get

(9.20)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
Q_{0}\gg B^{3/2}/P(B,T)\end{subarray}}|(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})(B,T,Q_{0})|\ll\sum_{P\gg 1}\frac{P^{\varepsilon}}{P^{\delta}}\ll 1.

On the other hand, by Lemma
9.16
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
Q_{0}\ll B^{3/2}/P(B,T)\end{subarray}}|\Sigma_{1}(B,T,Q_{0})|

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{T\geqslant 1}\frac{P^{-1/54}}{(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{T\geqslant 1}\frac{T^{-\delta/54}+(T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{\delta/54}}{(1+T^{2}/B^{1/2})^{A}}

\displaystyle\ll 1.

Hence

(9.21)

E_{3}(B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
Q_{0}\gg B^{3/2}/P(B,T)\end{subarray}}\Sigma_{2}(B,T,Q_{0})+O(1).

To evaluate
\Sigma_{2}
,
fix
S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
using Lemma
9.4
to get

\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}q^{\prime}=q\geqslant 1\\
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}\sum_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})/q^{\prime}\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})}S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\sum_{(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})+q^{\prime}\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})}\frac{I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})}{q^{2}}.

Let
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda^{\perp})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(\Lambda^{\perp})\leqslant\lambda_{3}(\Lambda^{\perp})

be the successive minima of
\Lambda^{\perp}=\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
.
Choose a matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda^{\perp}}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{3\times 6}

whose three row vectors form a shortest basis of
\Lambda^{\perp}
.
Consider the real density

(9.22)

\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}{(2\epsilon)^{-3}\int_{\mathbf{\Lambda^{\perp}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{3}}W(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},

where we view
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
as a column vector in
\mathrm{Mat}_{6\times 1}
.
If

(9.23)

\frac{q_{0}}{B}\geqslant A(W)\lambda_{3}(\Lambda^{\perp}),

where
A(W)>0
is a large constant defined in terms of
W
,
then by
[
44
]
*proof of Lemma 4.6,
which amounts to Poisson summation
and a Fourier-slice identity,
we have

(9.24)

q_{0}^{-3}\sum_{(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})+q^{\prime}\Lambda^{\perp}}I_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})B^{6}=\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}B^{3}\,h(q/Q,0),

where
(q_{0},q^{\prime})
corresponds to
what
[
44
]
calls
(n_{1},n_{0})
.
Strictly speaking, the proof requires
q_{0}/B

to exceed a certain constant times the length of the shortest basis

\mathbf{\Lambda^{\perp}}
of
\Lambda^{\perp}
.
However, this is equivalent to (
9.23
),
by Lemma
9.12
.

We can also interpret (
9.22
)
in terms of the orthogonal lattice
\Lambda
from (
9.1
).
By Poisson summation of
W(\frac{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}{H})

over
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Lambda
,
along the lines of
[
44
]
*Eq. (1.7),
we have

(9.25)

\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}=\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Lambda}W(\frac{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}{H})}{H^{3}}.

The actual interpretation we will use is (
9.26
),
a hybrid between (
9.22
) and (
9.25
).

Lemma 9.18
.

If
|t_{3}|\asymp|\mathbf{t}|
, say, then

(9.26)

\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W\left(x_{1},x_{2},-\frac{x_{1}t_{1}^{2}+x_{2}t_{2}^{2}}{t_{3}^{2}},s\mathbf{t}\right)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{t_{3}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Thus
\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\alpha\mathbf{t}),W}=|\alpha|^{-3}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
Moreover,

\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}\ll|\mathbf{t}|^{-3},\qquad\nabla_{\mathbf{t}}(\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W})\ll|\mathbf{t}|^{-4}.

Proof.

We may express
\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}

as a continuous function of
W\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
,
by (
9.22
),
after choosing a Leray form on
the smooth complete intersection
\mathbf{\Lambda^{\perp}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{6}
.
By continuity,
since
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\otimes C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

is dense in
C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
for the
\sup
norm,
it thus suffices to prove (
9.26
)
assuming that
W(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=W_{1}(\mathbf{x})W_{2}(\mathbf{y})
,
in which case the following three observations imply (
9.26
).
First, (
9.22
)
factors as a product of two densities,
one associated to the two-dimensional lattice
\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}^{2}=0
,
and the other associated the one-dimensional lattice
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{t}\mathbb{Z}
.
Second,

\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{t_{3}^{2}}
is a Leray form on
\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}^{2}=0
.
Third,

\frac{1}{H}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{t}\mathbb{Z}}W_{2}(\frac{\mathbf{y}}{H})\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}W_{2}(s\mathbf{t})\,\mathrm{d}s

as
H\to\infty
,
because if
f(s)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}W_{2}(s\mathbf{t})

then
\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}}f(\frac{h}{H})\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
.
The rest of Lemma
9.18
follows from (
9.26
).
∎

Among the four vectors

(\mathbf{t}^{2},\mathbf{0})
,

(\mathbf{0},(0,t_{3},-t_{2}))
,

(\mathbf{0},(t_{3},0,-t_{1}))
,

(\mathbf{0},(t_{2},-t_{1},0))

in
\Lambda^{\perp}
,
some three of them are linearly independent (e.g. the first three, if
t_{3}\neq 0
). Thus

(9.27)

\lambda_{3}(\Lambda^{\perp})\ll|\mathbf{t}|^{2},

which is optimal in view of the condition
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{t}^{2}\mathbb{Z}
in (
9.2
).

If
C_{1}B^{3/2}/P(B,T)\leqslant Q_{0}\leqslant C_{2}B^{3/2}
,
then
B^{1/2}/T^{2}\geqslant P^{1/\delta}\geqslant(C_{1}/C_{2})^{1/\delta}
, so

\frac{Q_{0}}{B}\geqslant\frac{C_{1}B^{1/2}}{P}\geqslant C_{1}P^{(1-\delta)/\delta}T^{2}\geqslant C_{1}(C_{1}/C_{2})^{(1-\delta)/\delta}T^{2}.

Hence, if
C_{1}
is large enough in terms of
W
,
then by (
9.27
) the condition (
9.23
) is satisfied
for all
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}
.
Thus by (
9.24
)

\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{0}q^{\prime}=q\geqslant 1\\
q_{0}\sim Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\phi(q_{0})}{q_{0}}\frac{q_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}h(q/Q,0)}{q^{2}B^{3}}\sum_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})/q^{\prime}\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})}S^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}).

Arguing as in

[
44
]
*proofs of Propositions 4.7 and 8.4,
we find that the average value of
q^{-4}S_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

over
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})/q\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
is

q^{-4}\sum_{a\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}\sum_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Lambda(\mathbf{t})/q\Lambda(\mathbf{t})}e_{q}(aF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))=q^{-4}\sum_{a\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}\sum_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\Lambda(\mathbf{t})/q\Lambda(\mathbf{t})}1=q^{-1}\phi(q),

and thus that the average value of
S^{\prime}_{q}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})

over
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})/q\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t})
is

\mathbf{1}_{q=1}
, by (
9.5
).
Therefore,

\Sigma_{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sum_{q\sim Q_{0}}\phi(q)\frac{\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}h(q/Q,0)}{B^{3}}.

We may sum over
q
using the
h
-function identity

\sum_{q\geqslant Q/L}\phi(q)h(q/Q,0)=Q^{2}(1+O(L^{-1}))\mathbf{1}_{L\geqslant 1},

which follows from
[
44
]
*Proposition 8.11
if
L\geqslant 1
,
and from the identity
h(q/Q,0)\mathbf{1}_{q\geqslant Q}=0

in
[
26
]
*Lemma 4
if
L\leqslant 1
.
Plugging our work into (
9.21
),
and bounding the total error term using the
inequality
\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}\ll|\mathbf{t}|^{-3}
,
we get

\begin{split}E_{3}(B)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
P(B,T)\gg 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}&\ll 1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
P(B,T)\gg 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\frac{|\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}|}{P(B,T)}\\
&\ll 1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\geqslant 1\\
P(B,T)\gg 1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{P(B,T)}\ll 1,\end{split}

similarly to (
9.20
).

For any integer
1\leqslant d\ll T
we have, by Lemma
9.18
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{t}\in d\mathbb{Z}^{3}\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{t}),W}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\sim T/d\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{u}),W}}{d^{3}}=\frac{1}{d^{3}}\left(\theta(T/d)+\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\asymp T/d\end{subarray}}O(|\mathbf{u}|^{-4})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right),

where for any
U>0
we let

\theta(U)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\sim U\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{u}),W}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}.

Moreover,

\theta(U)=\theta(1)

by scale-invariance of the volume form

\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp}(\mathbf{u}),W}\,d\mathbf{u}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Thus, by Möbius inversion,

2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{t}]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\\
|\mathbf{t}|\sim T\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\infty,\Lambda^{\perp},W}=\sum_{d\ll T}\frac{\mu(d)}{d^{3}}(\theta(1)+O(\frac{d}{T}))=\frac{\theta(1)}{\zeta(3)}+O(T^{-1}).

After writing the condition
P(B,T)\gg 1
in the form
1\ll T\ll B^{1/4}
, we get

E_{3}(B)=\sum_{1\ll T\ll B^{1/4}}\frac{\theta(1)}{2\zeta(3)}+O(1)=\frac{\theta(1)}{8\zeta(3)}\frac{\log{B}}{\log{2}}+O(1).

Plugging Lemma
9.18
into the definition of
\theta(1)
,
and changing variables from
\mathbf{t}
to
\mathbf{y}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}s\mathbf{t}
,
then integrating over
|s|\sim|\mathbf{y}|
,
where
s
may lie in either component of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,
we get

\begin{split}\theta(1)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{|s|\sim|\mathbf{y}|}W(x_{1},x_{2},-\frac{x_{1}y_{1}^{2}+x_{2}y_{2}^{2}}{y_{3}^{2}},\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{(y_{3}/s)^{2}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}}{|s|^{3}}\\
&=(2\log{2})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W(x_{1},x_{2},-\frac{x_{1}y_{1}^{2}+x_{2}y_{2}^{2}}{y_{3}^{2}},\mathbf{y})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{y_{3}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\\
&=(2\log{2})\sigma_{\infty},\end{split}

since
\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}}{y_{3}^{2}}
is a Leray form on the hypersurface
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Appendix A
Another example of
(\diamond)

Let
F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}y_{i}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
,
let
p\geqslant 3
be a prime and let
\psi:\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
be a non-trivial additive character.
Then by fixing
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
, we get

\begin{split}S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{a\neq 0}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\psi(aF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{y})\\
&=\sum_{a\neq 0}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\psi(ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x})\prod_{i=1}^{3}G(ax_{i},n_{i}),\end{split}

where

G(a,b)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}\psi(ay^{2}+by)=\psi\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{4a}\right)\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)G(1,0)\mathbf{1}_{a\neq 0}+p\mathbf{1}_{a=b=0}.

Evaluating the Gauss sums over
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{3}
,
we therefore find that if
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then

\begin{split}S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&=\sum_{ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0}\psi(ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x})\prod_{i=1}^{3}\psi\left(-\frac{n_{i}^{2}}{4ax_{i}}\right)\left(\frac{ax_{i}}{p}\right)G(1,0)\\
&=G(1,0)^{3}\sum_{ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0}\left(\frac{ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{p}\right)\psi\left(ax_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{n_{i}^{2}}{4ax_{i}}\right).\end{split}

By the Salié sum formula
in
[
31
, Lemma 12.4]
, we obtain

\displaystyle S(m,n;p)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{a\neq 0}\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\psi(ma+na^{-1})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{m}{p}\right)\sum_{v^{2}\equiv mn\bmod{p}}\psi(2v)G(1,0)\mathbf{1}_{m\neq 0}+\left(\frac{n}{p}\right)G(1,0)\mathbf{1}_{m=0}.

Applying this with
m=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0
and
n=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{n_{i}^{2}}{4x_{i}}
,
we get

\begin{split}S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&=G(1,0)^{4}\sum_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0}\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{p}\right)\psi(\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x})\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{p}\right)\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{v^{2}\equiv-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}\bmod{p}}\hskip-8.5359pt\psi(2v)\\
&=p^{2}\sum_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0}\sum_{u^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}}\psi(\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x}+u),\end{split}

where
u\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2v
.
Now, replacing
(\mathbf{x},u)
with
(t\mathbf{x},tu)

and averaging over
t\neq 0
, we get

\begin{split}S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})&=p^{2}\sum_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\neq 0}\sum_{u^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}}\frac{p\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x}+u=0}-1}{p-1}\\
&=p^{2}\frac{p(N_{1}-N_{2})-(N_{3}-N_{4})}{p-1},\end{split}

where

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{1}_{(\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x})^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}},\qquad N_{2}=\sum_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}=0}\mathbf{1}_{(\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{x})^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}},

\displaystyle N_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x},u}\mathbf{1}_{u^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}},\hskip 38.41139ptN_{4}=\sum_{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}=0}\sum_{u}\mathbf{1}_{u^{2}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{k}}.

If the projective conic in
N_{1}
is smooth (a condition defined by the non-vanishing of a
3\times 3
determinant, which is a sextic form in
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
),
then

\frac{N_{1}-1}{p-1}=\#\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p})=p+1,

so
N_{1}=p^{2}
.
Typically, the projective locus in
N_{2}
will be zero-dimensional, so that
N_{2}\ll p
.
The projective quadric surface in
N_{3}
has determinant
n_{1}^{2}n_{2}^{2}n_{3}^{2}/4
,
so if
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\neq 0
then

\frac{N_{3}-1}{p-1}=\#\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})+p,

so
N_{3}=p^{3}+(p^{2}-p)=p^{3}+O(p^{2})
.
The projective locus in
N_{4}
is one-dimensional, so
N_{4}\ll p^{2}
.
Thus, typically,

S_{p}(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\ll p^{2}\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}\ll p^{3}.

This justifies the claim for
(c_{1},c_{2})=(-1,0)

in Example
1.3
(4).
